Tea a ld Sir Andrew Clarke.

For years, whenever my Customers have asked me for a Mild Tea. I have advised the Pure. old Fashioned China which I eep, and the advice has been appreciated. It is very satisfactory to them that such an eminent authority as Sir Andrew Clarke, the London Physician, corroborates

keep,

_ opinion.

Here lean extract from an interesting lecture, delivered by Sir Andrew the other day, to the Students of the London

Hospital

"Tea is a blessed beverage. Ido not know what I should do without it. But there is tea and tea, and one of the teas I have in my mind is the representation of all that is physiologically wicked. Igo about town a good deal holding consulta- tions here and there, and about five o'clock, when I get into a place, the lady of the house will say to me : 'Sir Andrew, you look tired, do let me give you a cup ot tea.' I say thank you very much.' But the tea has stood for half-an-hour, and she remarks, • I know you do you not like it strong, Sir Andrew. and she then pours about a tablespoonful of the tea into a cup and fills thecup up with water. Now I call it positivecruelty to give tea like that to anybody. and I hope you, gentlemen, will always set your face against it. Tea, to be useful, should be first of all Black China Tea ; the Indian Tea which is being cultivated has become so powerful in its effects upon the nervons system that a cup of it taken early in the morning, as many do, so disorders the nervous system that those who take it actually get into a state of tea intoxication, and it produces a form of nervous disturbance, which is most painful to witness. If you want to have, either for yourselves or for your patients, tea which will not injure and which will refresh, get Black China Tea, putting in the right measure—the Old fashioned teaspoonful for each person and one for the blessed pot. Then pour on briskly boiling water, and within five minutes you must pour it off again, or it will become wicked instead of good." Such is Sir Andrew Clarke's opinion and advise. A few words of warning, however, are necessary to those who have for- gotten what this old fashioned tea is like. The colour of the tea after it is brewed will be found tobe much paler than that now in use, and the taste may not be acceptable at first owing to its apparent insipidness ; but if its use is persisted in, it will very soon be found to be much and if, in a week to two after its use, a trial is again made of the kind now in ordinary use, you wmiolrlewsuointadbelrehfoowr etvheers yy,;t:m' Could This old fashioned China Tea, as recommended by Sir Andrew Clarke, can be obtained now, as always or the last 35 co have been using it,and you will endorse Sir Andrew's statement. years, at

WHAT is that which Adam never saw, never pos- sessed, and yet he gave two to each of his children! —Parts. A GASESTER finding luck rather hard against him, exelaimed, " Ah, Fortune ! 'tis true you make me lose, but I defy you to make me pay.' A THIRSTY Kentish elector, at an inn, in Maid- stone, shouted, "Waiter, bring me bitter beer !" —" We can bring you a bit o' bread," said the waiter, "but not a bit o' beer." "Is there any danger from the boa constriclor ?" said a visitor to the Zoological Gardens a feways since.—" Not the least, sir ; he never bites, he swallows his victuals whole." IN the newspaper account of an inquest held on the body of a glutton, who died by devouring part of a goose, the verdict, suffocation, was printed, with more truth than was intended, stuffocation. THERE was a rule in an old debating society which might be advantageously recommended to some of our public bodies—" That any gentleman wishing to speak the whole evening should have room to himself." NATURE AND ART. —A lecturer, addressing a Hampshire audience, contended, with tiresome prolixity, that Art could not improve Nature • until one of his hearers, losing all patience, set tht room in a roar by exclaiming, "How would you look without your wig ? " A CONNECTICUT Jonathan, in taking a walk with his dearest, came to a tollbridge, when he, as honest as he was wont to be, said, after paying his toll (one cent), "Come, Suke, you must pay your own toll, for just as like as not I sha'nt have you arter all."

AN English lady on arriving at Calais, on her way to make a grand tour, was surprised and somewhat indignant at being termed for the first time in her life "a foreigner."—" You mistake, madam," said she to the libeller, with some pique, "it is you who are foreigners, we are English. THE equivocality of many of the names of places in Scotland has gi mu occasion to a very amusing saying regarding a clergyman. "He was born in the parish of Dull, brGught up in the school of Dome, and finally settled as minister in thl parish of Drone " A GENTLEMAN was describing to his children some remarkably tine apples which he had seen in a friend's orchard. " rhey are almost as large," said he, "as Urban's head, allowing a little for hyperbole."—" 'Hyperbole !" said Urban, "what is that, papa? is it my hair ?" _ _

During the time the late Sir Robert Peel was Premier, Lady Peel was in the habit of pasting all the articles which appeared in the newspapers against him on a screen. "Well," observed a friend, there ig nothing very singular in that—it is but the duty of every good wife to screen her husband's faults." Two men in dispute reflected upon each other's veracity. One of them replied that "lie was never whipped but once by his father, and that was for telling the truth."—" I believe, then," retorted the other, "the truth was whipped out of you, for you have never spoken it since.'' BIKE the generality of kings and conquerers, Frederick the Great had a most philosophic indiffer- ence to death—hi others. In one of ids battles a battalion of veterans having taken to their heels, he galloped after them, bawling cut—" Why do you run away, you old blackguards? Do you want to live for ever ? " BOYLE tells us of a great traveller, who being rallied upon his rambling humour, amwered, that he would cease travelling as soon as he could find a country where power and credit were in the hands of honest men, and perferment went by merit. Query i—lf such a man were now alive, where would he take up his abode? FEMININE PIIRASEOLOGY.—Ignorence of female modes of speech often leads to great misunder- standing. A lady who says—" I would not make a fright of myself," means, generally, that she neglects nothing, however minute, to make herself attractive. With another—" to be decent" means to have a mass of beautiful lace, and a little for- tune in diamonds. SMITHERS, on going home the other night, was run against by a three-storey house which was chasing a lamp-post. On coming to he thus reasoned with himself :—" Is that mud (hiccup) or is it brains (hiccup) ? If it's mud I'm mortally 'toxicated ; if it's brains I'm slightly dead (hiccup), that's all." When we left he was trying to persuade the porch of a house that it was unconstitutional to leave awning-posts out of doors after nightfall. A GENTLEMAN once introduced his son to Row- land Hill, by letter, as a youth of great promise, and likely to do honour to the University of which he was a member; "but he is shy," added the father, "and idle and I fear buries his talents in a napkin." A short time afterwards the parent, anxious for the reverend gentleman's opinion, inquired what he thought of his son? "I have shaken the napkin," said Rowland Hill, "at all =niers, and there is nothing in it l" AN UNREDEEMED PLEDGE. —Sixty-Seven years ago, a committee of the House of Commons was appointed to examine into the state of steam naviga- tion. Lord Stanley was chairman of that com- mittee ; and on Mr. Stephenson, the eminent engineer, speaking of the probability of steam-ships creasing the Atlantic, Lord Stanley rose from his seat and exclaimed, "Good heavens ! what do you say? If steam-ships cross the Atlantic, I will eat the boiler of the first boat MILD Piaras.—An old lady visiting Southport for her health was desired to take a few colocynth pills occasionally. She put some in a. saucer in the cupboard at her lodgings, and thought, after she had taken some a few days, that they were very mild and serviceable. She recommended some to another old lady, and on reaching a saucer down, the stranger was surprised to find holes through the pills, and on examination it was found that the old lady had been takiug for mild pills the landlady's daughter's black beads THE DOUBLE MISFORTUNE. —At a provincial theatre in France, a comedy was played entitled Twenty-six Misfortunes of Harlequin. An actor who happened to be among the spectators, called to Harlequin at the end of the play—" I say, the play-bill is a cheat. I paid to see twenty-six mis- fortunes ; I have counted well on my fingers, and there were but twenty-four ! "—" Eh! Sango- demi I" shouted Harlequin, "did not I marry at the end of the comedy? And does not that make up at least two misfortunes ? "- AN EXTREMELY USEFUL DISCOVERV.—An Irish gentleman h-is invented a highly ingenious method by which, for the small charge of one penny, any one can, at any rime, have his boots cleaned. The old plan, lately so much in vogue, of flapping the dust off with your handkerchief, is entirely super. ceded. The method is this : " Von hail a penny 'bus, and sceatnble to the seat farthest from the door, taking care to drag your feet against the dresses of all the ladies. On getting out you will find your hoots have been perfectly cleaned for one penny. N.B. Silk dresses make the beat polishers. A velvet dress (if there should happen to be a Jewess inside) is the most effectual for wiping off the mud." WHY WE GET DRUNK.

BY MR. H. WHITHAM.

I do not propose in this article to deal with drunkenness from the total ahstainer's point of view. As may have been seen from what I have already said, I look upon the abstainer, more especially the one who boasts be never in his life touched, tasted, or handled intoxicating liquors, as being somewhat of a fanatic, in his methods of propaganda. essommola

I:have, all my life, worked with men who were great drunkarcs ; men who spent all the time they could possibly spare from bed and work in the public-house ; men who never in the least recognized the rights, and claims of either wife or children on their earnings, and cultivated most tenderly the great fallacy, that what they earned was their own, and "had they not a right to do as they liked with their own ?" This fallacy does not attach itself to the drunkard alone, but is one that is entertained by many of those in high places, hence, possibly, the reason of the drunkard's existence.

The drunkard, like the glutton, has habituated his stomach to taking in excess what may be good for him. Some of us could never drink or eat to excess. Our nerves, stomach, ability, constitution, and poor heads would so stoutly resist repeated states of intoxication, that we should have to give it up from sheer inability to carry it on. Doctors recognize the uselessness of trying to make people sober by law, and in consequence take no leading part in such methods. Men engaged in hard physical work, in-door or out-door, make the most con- firmed drunkards. When it is otherwise, it may be noticed that the victim to tho habit is generally one of a well developed animal and physical nature. So men engaged out-door in the cold, or in-door in extreme heat can dispose of a deal more liquor than can men engaged in a moderate temperature and sedentary work.

AU efforts to cure drunkenness, other than social efforts, have failed because it has never been recognized that to make a drunkard, a certain soil, or constitution, was needed in which the taste for liquor might develope. There are men who abominate the taste or smell of tobacco, let it come in ever so fair a shape. I see men every day where I work pull out the snuff box, and with a little spoon insert between the lower gum, and lip a spoonful of snuff. I may say this is done by women, as well as men. For women to make a pill of soap and snuff and insert it between the gum and lip ensures them white teeth, so they think. These are habits that men and women, take to very much as a duck takes to water.

Does any one imagine for a moment that law can cure these habits? A man may abstain by exerting his will power, or so long as a fever of enthusiasm is upon him, but our experience proves that neither of these features is sufficient. One who has had the "D.T.'s" three or four times is a lost man in every sense. In fact the manhood is gone out of him, and law must here step in, and the poor helpless mortal should be placed under restraint. To take such a one to the police court is simply a travesty of justice. Those interested in this question will wotice that there is springing up a class of specia- lists that recognize most of the above features, and deal with drunkenness as a disease which, it governed at all by the thinking powers of the patient, must be EO only slightly.

- What may be done with the child unfortunately de- veloping a drink habit I may not go into, more than by saving that I am sure it can, like consumption, be cured if 'taken in its very earliest stages. Train and develsp the lungs, and you cure consumption. In a somewhat similar way deal with a child developing a taste for alcohol, and you may make it almost impossible for a child to become a drunkard. I think those who have studied this subject are pretty well satisfied that either separately or combined hereditary tendency and envi- ronment are the chief, almost only, causes of drunken- ness. With the former of these causes I am not the right person to deal. It is of the latter I want to say a few words.

Drunkenness is more an effect than a cause. It does ' not cause poverty so much as poverty causes it. It is the state of mind a man habitually carries with him that causes him to squander his money, and to become a drunkard. I would support any and all restrictions that could be placed upon drink, as a matter of working , policy, but I have for years been in favour of the free, and unrestricted sale of liquor, and I am satisfied now that if this policy were adopted there would be fewer drunkards in 20 years' time than there would be with all the Local Option laws that could be passed. There are restrictions on poisons, morphia and opium, yet who- ever wants any of these drugs can get them. For gain, highly respectable druggists will enable any man either to have a shot, or a quietus. These restrictions act like the Maine prohibition law. The victim simply gets a larger private supply, and has a bigger " ‘..'"unk." The true remedy is not to restrict, but to destroy the desire ' for drink. I have pointed these features in the liquor question out, because they are existent, and the cure because I think it possible. . .

But there are other causes steadily at work that the reformer may grapple with. We were told, ages ago, to ;0 drink and forget our misery. Suppose we set this in- junction on one side, and try to remove the misery. It gets more true every day that " Man sighs to think how oft he's found Life's warmest welcome at an inn.' In the home and the workshop a great deal can be done to make a sober people. Itis an alarming fact that all classes of people seem to be rushing into the vortex of drink. The hurry and scurry of trade, the excitement consequent on the rushing after riches which pervades the people generally causes the longing for something " just to settle the nerves." The possession of wealth without the necessary training as to its responsibilities causes drunkenness. The possession of wealth, and the consequent increase of an immense number of people of both sexes who lead a life of idle unproductive useless— ness, is making drunkards. At the door of the Primrose League lies a heavy charge in this respect.

But more to the point is the fact that we do not make- homes in the truest and best sense. We allow, for the mere sake a gain, holes to exist in which no man,.' returning from a bard day's work,can find any comfort. ' What wonder if a man does seek the comforts of the tap-room,when the only home be has is such as has been freely described in the Warrington Press? The greatest curse in the world is the institution of female factory ! labour. We seem to have encouraged a false standard ' of liberty, and girls flock into factories, and other work- shops in preference to domestic service. They seem to think that the liberty they have in the evenings and on Sundays is all-sufficient, and the criminal system of competition encourages their entrance into these places. I There is not a doubt that the ignorance of domestic. duties fostered thus during girlh-od is a primary cause of intemperance. Her ignorance prevents her from doing those things that would make her home a place of rest, a refug.e P4r her husband and children. Her ignorance prevents her from buying and preparing the kind of food that would give nourishment and satisfy the cravings of hunger, which drive the inmates of the house to stimulants, to silence. longings the causes of which are unknown to him. Teach every girl the hygiene of foods, and you have gone far towards making a home of peace and happiness, because one great source of intemperance has been removed.

The baby girl should be taught as she grows along wi9i the years, to cultivate a nataral taste for the beau- tif 11. In her home she should be approached on every side with all that is elevating to the character. Books should be her greatest pleasure as a recreation, and study ; pictures, not daubs, should be there, not merely as so much furniture, but as a retex of the beautiful in her own mind. Music, of which the poets have written so much, has so much power in making home beautiful that it should be the leading feature in school-girl edu- cation. Here in the States it is being made much of as an influence in the temperance cause. The education ' which it was fondly hoped would make the people sober has been somewhat disappointing,because it has touched too much the commercial and not enough the altruistic side of our nature. We have been looking for the age of brain and of heart to come in, hoping to see the brute go out, but how can it be, when as Tennyson wrote " City children soak and blacken soul and sense in cite slime?" Temperance reformers may give their money, time, and energy, but it is all in vaiii so long as " Crime and hunger cast our an,dclens by the ths.usand on is street ?"

I plead for the workingman and his home. I know, as :-prncer has taught, that " excess of bodily exercise L.naiinishes the power of thought," and that "in peasants who spend their lives in muscular labour the activity of mind is very small." Modern civilization is failing because it is reducing men, women, and children to the function of machines, a life so monotuncus and sordid, so destitute of anything that refines and elevates the human spirits, that the only wonder hi We are as sober as we give ourselves credit for being. I would ask all educated persons, and all the wealthy reformers to put themselves in the places of those I have written of, and answer the question what would they be other than such as we are.

I offer no protest against the present work of temper- ance reformers. All I say is, it does not grasp the whole situation. I believe, with Miss Willard—a name that should be familiar to every temperance advocate—that he children must first be saved, and taught not to drink. That woman must be drawn more into all social life. "Women more in the State,not less at home, man more in the home, not less, in the State." Miss Willard thinks that when woman reaches her full place in citizenship, her full and rightful position in the life of the world, the temperance cause will then have a chance. Then we shall commerce humanizing the con- ditions of labour. We shall then know the difference between cause, and the effect.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, FEBRUARY 4, 1893.

Fullam for South Meath. His rising was received I with prolonged and enthusiastic cheers. A glance at the Prime Minister gave the lie direct to the many sinister rumors respectng his health. There he stood at the table with his hands on the box, erect, cheerful, and smartly clothed. His voice was in magnificent tone, and the testimony of political friends and of political opponents agreed in describ- ing his speech on Mr. Macartney's motion, and his speech in reply to 'Air. Balfour's criticisms, as remarkable manifestations of physical and intellect- ual vigor.

Mr. Balfour did not enter the House till twenty minutes past four. His followers welcomed him with a demonstation which was evidently pre- arranged, as Mr. Balfour was for some time convers- ing at the fireplace in the Opposition lobby waiting for the opportune moment for an appearance in the House. The duel between Mr. Balfour and Mr. Gladstone was watched from the Ladies' Gallery with absorbing interest by Mr. Balfour's sister, who sat beside Mrs. Gladstone and Miss Helen Gladstone. Miss Mathew, a daughter of the judge whose con- duct was assailed by Mr. Balfour and vindicated by Mr. Gladstone, was in the front row of the Ladies' Gallery.

" CAPITAI " CHAT.

Sir Charles Gavan Duffy, the late Premier of Victoria, who is at present resining at the Villa Frenchinelli, his beautifnl home in the Eiviera, is engaged in the preparation of a further volume on Carlyle, and in writing his own biography. He returns to London in May to take the control of an important literary enterprise. Sir Charles has now entered on his seventy-seventh year.

There is a row in New Zealand over the following item in the Government accounts :—" Expenses in connection with the hamper of game sent to her Majesty, £22 is. od." It turns out that a new Knight—Sir Patrick Buckley, the Colonial Secre- tary—sent this little present to the Queen soon after receiving his title, but the Radicals naturally object very strongly to the country being called upon to pay for Ministers' gifts to Windsor Castle kitchen. By expressing his gratitude at other people's cost, Sir Patrick seems to have pretty fairly gauged the value of his title.

The death of Mrs. Palmer-Morewood, of Alfreton- park, Derby, snaps a link with the great literary past, for that lady was the second daughter of George Anson, Lord Byron, who succeeded his first cousin, the poet, as seventh holder of the title bestowed, in 1643, upon the ancient family of Byron. Mrs. Palmer-Morewood was the younger sister of Mrs. Coulson, who married in 1834 Mr. John Coulson, of Blenkinsopp Castle, Northumberland, and was, therefore, the aunt of the well-known actor, who is the husband of Miss Kate Phillips,and who, though his name, is Coulson, is familiar to players and to playgoers as "handsome Harry Conway.'

Mr. Arnold Frank Hills, the defendant in the libel action in which Dr. T. R. Allinson was the plaintiff, is to be the Conservative candidate for South West Ham at the next election. He will be a strong opponent to Mr. Hier-Hardie, as he is the managing director of the Thames Iron Works,many of the employes of which reside in the constituency. Mr. Hills, as president of the Vegetarian Society and proprietor ot the "Vegetarian," has taken an active part for years now in the warfare against flesh foods. Like Keir-llardie, he is also a great temper- ance man ; whilst another bent of his mind is indi- cated by his membership of the Church of England Purity Society.

Sir Ricbard,Owen, the ?great naturalist, leaves a fortune of nearly £34,000, and considerable real property to his heirs. Science seems, like poetry, to be fairly lucrative now-a-days. The real estate, which is near Durham, goes to his eldest grandson, while his daughter-in-law, the widow of his eldest son, who met a somewhat tragic death a few years ago, enjoys the life-interest of the personalty, that is should she remain unmarried. Mrs. Owen has lived in Sheen Lodge—which, by the way, the Queen has given her the use of for her life—since she lost her husband, and her unceasing and assid- uous care doubtless helped to prolong the venerable naturalist's life.

At the National Liberal Club yesterday three more portraits were added to the large dining- room, and all the five panels of the rooms are now filled. The portraits include : Mr. Gladstone, painted by Mr. Collin Forbes, member of the Cana- 1 dian Academy ; Lord Granville, a replica of the picture presented to the Earl by the Liberal Peers in the House of Lords: Viscount Oxenbridge, painted by Mr. Hermann Herkomer, and unveiled last week—Mr. Bright and Mr. Cobden. These last two are replicas of original paintings by Mr. Lowes Dickenson. These portraits have all been presented to the club through the efforts of the Art Committee, and have been placed in position to be ready for the conversazione which is to be held on the Bth of Feb.

Miss Helen Sheffield, sister of young Sir Berkeley Sheffield. of Normanby Park, Lincolnshire, whom Lord Arthur Grosvenor, eldest surviving son of the Duke of Westminister, is shortly to marry, is a descendant, though with the bar sinister in the escutcheon, of that ancient family of SheEeld who were Earls of Mulgrave and the Dukes of Norman- by many years before the titles of Mulgrave and of Normanby (marquis) were bestowed upon the Phipp's who now hold them. On the death of the second Duke of Normanby in 1735 the titles became extinct, and the estates all passed to the illegitimate son of the first duke, who assamed the surname of Sheffield as directed in the will, and, haying married a daughter of that distinguished officer, General Sabine, was created a baronet in 1755. The present baronet is the sixth holder of the title, and is just seven and twenty.

Mr. W. L. Watkinson, who is Tying dangerously ill of pheumonia, is the wittiest cleric in the Wes- leyan Church, as Mr. P. W. Macdonald, the brother- in-law of Mr. Lockwood Kipling, Ur. Burne-Jones, and Mr. Poynter, is the most humorous. His bent for caustic epigram is apt to become a snare, and in point of fact he is obliged sometimes to struggle to repress the temptation to be brilNant under in, auspicious conditions. Mr. Watkinsen is to succeed the veteran Dr. Gregory in the autumn as official editor of Wesleyan publications. He does not share the distrust of modern criticism which has led some elderly ecclesiastiastics to seek: unavailingly to impeach the doctrinal integrity of the chief theological divines in the Methodist communion. The heresy-hunt now being pursued in that quar- ter is therefore likely to die a natural death as soon as this genial successor of Sidney Smith assumes The reins of power.

Although the electric installation in Windsor Castle is to remain, there is no probability of its being extended, for the present at leant, to any of I the rooms usually in the oacupation of her Majesty. I At present there are glow lamps along one section of the corridor, and also in the billicrd-room, an apartment to which the- Queen never resorts. Everything has been done, by means of tinted shades and the like, to subdue the glare which is found so objectionable. When her Maiesty passes from the oak dining room to her private staircase she has within almost simnitaneous view examples of the four systems of artiacial light in common use. The outside lamps which illumine the dark- ness of the quadrangle are supplied with gas by the Windsor Corporation. The corridor is lit up with electric lamps, intermingled with old-fashioned lamps, both pedestal, table,and bracket,. containing oil'. in her own rooms candles as well as oil are employed, the chamber formerly occugied by the late Prince Consort being in fact never suffered to I remain in darkness.

The sermon was in great mea- sure a stern and spirited rebuke to the wealthy idlers of the West-end—not J. K. Jemme's club a intellectual idlers,but the fellows with more money than brains. Their wcrk ahould largely consist ot dispensing the wealth, of which they were the te-Mporary custodians in :the service and to the benefit of their fellow-cnea- , tures.

Army Recruiting Department have won, the warm commendation of the Commander-in-Chiei and Mr., Campbell-Banner- man, is a brother of the late Earl of Denbigh and uncle of the present peer. He passed through the Staff College with distinction, and was appointed military attache at the French headquarters during the Franco-Prussian War. Napoleon ILL created him a Knight of th2,Legion of Honor. Ha-ring been born at the Court of William 117., where his father was Master of the Horse to Queen Adelaide, it fol- lows that he is every inch a courtier, as becomes an ex-page of honor. But General Feilding is also an industrious man of affairs, much interested in colo- nial matters and colonisation farms in Queensland and New Zealand, and as skilful with his brush as he is with his pen. Tall and shapely in figure. his thoughtful, handsome face may often be met in Pall-mall. His cosy chambers in Sloane-st. are a museum of art treasures from the East, and here it is that the General works, when not engaged at the Horse Guards, at his philanthropic schemes for benefiting poor ladies and distressed soldiers, for he has great sympathy for all unfortunate folk. The General is fifty-six and still a bachelor, hale and hearty.

MEN AND MATRIMONY. SOME INTERESTING INFORMATION.

That the mothers and daughters of England are greatly to be pitied does not admit of a doubt. For years has the plaintive wail arisen that men will not dance and will not marry. They do not object to making love—in fact, many like the pro- cess ; but matrimony is quite another matter. How , often have we heard a lover sigh, " She's the dearest girl, the only girl I ever really loved ; but look at my income—." Perhaps while the said income is in process of expansion the roses flit from the dear one's cheeks. In order that men and women may know how the question actually stands, I have digested masses of matrimonial statistics extending over several years, and the results thereof I humbly present here. I will en- deavour to show what likelihood there is of a man don- ning the matrimonial fetters at various ages, and also indicate at what period of life his hymeneal tendency is greatest. My facts are drawn from the upper or pru- dent classes of society, because among the artisan and labouring population the same restrictions are not felt.' If a man's income does not vary much between twenty and fifty, he will probably marry at the earlier age. Among commercial and professional people the condi- tions are different, and a man rarely takes upon himself the responsibility of a wife and a possible family until his means or prospects appear to justify the stop. Also, any rise in the standard of comfort or in the price of necessaries still further delay the happy event. Take 1.000 young men at the age of 20 and übserve them again on the attainment of the quarter-century. In the five years only 21 will have married. In spite, therefore, of five springs, during which the "young men's fancy fondly turns to thoughts of love,"on an aver- age only 4 1-5 fond fancies are wedded. If, on the other hand, we take 1,000 at 25, and watch their proceedings during the next quinquennium, we shall find that no less than 94 find mates. If I had made similar observations upon the same number of bachelors from 30 to 85 years old. I should have been gratified by attending 133 wed- dings. This is the way the table runs :

20-25 2l marriages 50-55 43 marriages. 25-30 94 marriages 55-60 34 marriages. 30-35 l3B marriages 60-65 l9 marriages. 35-40 147 marriages 65-70 5 marriages. 40 45 122 rnarrinnes 70-75 3 marriages. 45-50 79 marriages 75 80 1 a marriage. It must be remembered that the above refers only to bachelors.

A careful study of the table will be to give benefit to marriageable maidens. Warned by it, they will not waste sweet smiles and gentle glances upon young men under 30. These will be relegated to the society of dowagers and aged spinsters, while the men from 30 to 45 years old will receive the maximum of a feminine attention. Between 35 and 40 i 3 the best age. Under the present system, since only 1 in 543 of young men under 25 get married, the amount of fliftation indulged in by the other 49 must be truly awful. The fraction opposite the ages 75-80 does not mean that an elderly bridegroom invariably retires before the marriage ser- vice is completed, but implies that only I out of 2000, gay young bachelors of 75 takes unto himself a wife during the next five years. The competition to secure that one must be severe.

It will not surprise anyone to learn that a, widower is more inclined to marry than a bachelor of the same age. A widower has tried the experiment, and knows the delights of a vie-a-vie at dinner. He also has a house and perhaps a family on his hands. Thhin, 1,000 widowers and treating them as we did the bachelors, we shall have :- 45-50 110 marriages. 65-70 36 marriages. 50-55 93 marriages. 70-75 ll3tmarriages. 55-6 a 75 marriages. 75-8 60-65 57 marri-ages. It will be noticed at once that the pursuit ora widower of between 50 and 56 will furnish as good results as that of a bachelor 25 years younger. Think of the trained and tried affections of the widower,and do not hesitate. A bacheor over 60 is no good at all, but a. widower presents excellent opportunities ; while above '75 the bachelor is literally nowhere. It gives one an increased respect.for. human nature to think of ten widowers at this advanced age tottering to the altar for every one shrinking experimentalist. When the unmarried' reflect , upon thesethings, and upon the courage of their experi- enced brethren who dare not once but many times to embark upon the matrimonial voyage, we think that a great impetus will be given to the hymeneal market. Ono thinrhas at any rate been established—to wit, that no men can be called a " confirmed bachelor until he attains at least the half-century. Rien then there are strong hopes of ultimate repentance. It is the conduct of those young men in the twenties that gives us-the keeneat pain.—"-Pall Mall Gazette."

A PRIEST EATEN BY WOLVES.

A terrible wolf' story is reported from Balgrade. A pope was driqiing in a aledge in broad daylight from Svilajuay to Svilajuar when a herd of wolves burst out of a wood. The pope fired into the wolves, and one dropped dead, whilst the others ran away. The pope was anxious to secure is booty, andwith his servant went to fetch the wolf. As he dropped it into the sledge the horses, who, were trembling,on account of the wolves, shied and ran away. The pope cried tc, the servant, " You must catch the horses, or else we are both lbst. My ri±e is in the sledge." The young man ran for dear life, but it took him a tong time to catch the frightened animals. When he had driven back with them, to, the spot where he had left thepope he found the top, boots with legs and feet in them, blood-stained tattered clothes, arid the long grey beard of his master.

LADIES' LETTER.

Mlt DEAR NELLTE, The cry of sales has gone up exultantly from hundreds of women throughout the land during the past week or two, for if there is one thing more dear to the feminine soul than another, it is the purit of bargains. Twice &Tear, with feverish excttement, women indulge in the exciting chase; but it is especially at this season, when the days are dull and the glamour of gaslight is thrown over the tempting piles of dress length , and ribbons, laces and remnants, that sales exercise their greatest fascinations. Without 'salons one stands but a poor chance in the hurry and rush and scramble of a winter sale. The mild and timid

_ little woman is very soon beaten into the back- ground. Goods are ruthlessly snatched from her hand as soon as she lays an irresolute finger upon them. She is elbowed and pushed and driven from thezost likely parcels of goods. Tossed like a corir in a surging sea, she makes frantic and ineffectual attempts to get near the laces, or find an odd length of brocade; and at length, dis- hevelled and exhausted, she makes her way out with a few wholly useless articles, seized upon faute de mieux, or from sheer, weariness or help- lessness to get what she wants.

Bonnets are growing smaller and beautifully lest, and by the time spring is with us they will have disappeared altogether if they continue to decrease in size as they have hitherto done. A tiny "calotte " of jet or gold embroidery, with a tuft of feathers or flowers, a flounce of lace, or an edging of plume and the bonnet is ready for outdoor wear. Wings, however, bats' wings and birds' wings in jet, or charming green stones like light- toned erneraldii, are the novelty of the day, and for theatre or matinee wear exceptionally brilliant green is a very prevalent and fashionable colour, and green dresses,-I am told, are likely to be a lead- ing feature of the coming spring season. .A few of the detest walking and house-dresses are being made with the double-skirt; a rather thick cloth is the material usually chosen, and the upper skirl, -which reaohes to the knees, is left with the rage unhemmed. Princesso gowns ere fashionalle— I well worn," as the phrase goes—but are not very general on account gf the undeniable fact that.tbey require first-class cutting, and are even then only becoming to perfect figures. A charming travelling dress of extreme simplicity was DE smooth-faced Venetian cloth of lavender grey. The plain but rather wide skirt had all its fulness gathered into the back, and the front was worked in a conventional floral design in thick white- silk twist. This same embroidery was carried up the sides of the skirt and was also used to form entirely the deep corselet of the bodice, which was met below the bust and shoulders with a yoke of white goide flecked with irregular stars of lavender silk embroidery. 4 full puff of grey extended from the shoulder to the elbow of the slee7es, and from thence they were made of white voile fitting closely to the wrist. The toque accompanying this dress was of white cloth flecked with grey. A wide ruche of grey ostrich feather trimming rested upon the hair, sad in front was a cluster of upstanding white wings.

Alloys me to direct your attention to the new monthly magazine for young people The Young Gentiewman a product, as the title implies, of that favourite journal of ours The Gentle:roman. The former magazine is particularly attractive for the younger generation, especially the illustrations, and its features are far ahead of those all ready in the Held that eater for youthful audiences, A group of charming lady writers succeed in mak:ng its literary matter of exceptional excellence. The crinoline is quite the talk of the town. It now turas out that we were never really in dangey of its revival. No smart woman evir .thought of wearing one, any more than of walking down Bond. street in a low-necked dress, which in the year 1841 would not have been at all remarkable. The pale was taken up in sad affright by those Wto gave credence to reports without draly examining the foundation for them. Had the crinoline appeared at a hunt ball, church parade, or a good wedding, instead of in a window in Regent-street, we might have .been justified in feeling a little alarm. As L, is we are positively and delightfully secure against any invasion of steels, canes, and buckram. -

Velvet arLd velveteen keep general favourites for wear, and as the season advances they will be much 111010 in evidence. A really beautiful toilette de villa I saw the other day in velvet, of velours do theme or corduroy. The colour was aubergine, and the material used for yoke, under.sleeves, and broad band on skirt was the same colour, closely braided in black and gold. Yours sincerely,

But such a revival would certainly imply the utter failure of the rational dress reformers to influence permanently their sister women's tastes and ideas in dress. _ _

Queen Victoria sticks to old Patterns.

Queen Victoria, as may be expected, is not given to alterations as regards her toilet, and makes the old pat-

terns last. Her necklaces, earrings, and bracelets remain as they were in the beginnirg, and the one and only innovation in her jewel chest is that little crown made to replace a much more sensible and becoming diadem which was always worn by her Majesty before her widowhood.

A Good Example.

The Princess of Wales, especially for all country house pursuits, dresses plainly with extreme strictness, de- pending for the distinguished aspect of her own and her daughters' costumes on the perfection of cut, style,- and Bt. Even bands of velvet or of fur are seldom used to trim the gowns of these Royal ladies. Dainty Table Decoration. One of the prettiest centrepieces at a breakfast was a round Leghorn hat filled with roses. The dish holding water was set in the crown, and a pink satin ribbon was passed around it through the centre of the flowers, and was tied in a bow en one side of the brim. The ribbon was twisted where it passed through the flowers, that it might not separate them. It looked as though a garden hat had been used as a basket while the roses were being gathered, and the effect was most artistic.

DARK WOOD.

A TALE OF LONEMOOR BT MRS. HARRIETT LEWIS, Author of "The Sundered Hearts," "The Bailiff's Scheme," "The Double Life," &c.

CHAPTER VIII. AN ADMIRER.

Mrs. Quillet was sitting in an easy chair in a most uneasy attitude when Gwendoline entered. The woman rose at once and made a very low bow, and then looked beyond the girl with a visible impatience. Seventeen years had greatly changed the old housekeeper. She was more prim and reserved than of old, and she seemed like one who broods always over some recent trouble. In truth, she mourned still for lier lost Miss Clare, and of late the problem of Gwendoline's future had begun to distress her. ' The mystery of the girl's existence, the sorrow and bitter shame of that fatal winter seventeen years ago, were pressing very heavily upon her this morn- ing, and she dreaded to see ler charge, and reiter- ated in her own soul that wish of long ago, that the child had died in her early years! It was quite plain that the good dame did not recognise the girl, and a bright smile chased away Gwendoline's tears, as she exclaimed— " Don't you know me, Mi s. Quillet?—dear Nurse Quillet? Don't you know little Gwen ?',k Mrs. Quillet started back in amazemerTt. • Was this "little Gwen "—Clare Markham's name- less child? This girl with her royal splendour of loveliness, her rare and radiant beauty? This "little Gwen f' The girl laughed again at her surprise, and bestowed upon her a warm embrace, the old woman submitting in a sort of stupefied silence. Then a porter announced that the vehicle was waiting, and they were driven rapidly towards the station, throughout the drive the old housekeeper scarcely removing her gaze from ihe girl's counten- ance. It's beauty startled her. She was like the hen who had hatched a swan. What was she to do with this young creature? What life had she to offer her? She began to think that in training the girl as a lady she had committed a terrible mistake.

For the girl was nameless, of strange origin, and unless the squire should adopt her or leave her something in his will, she would have to earn her own living. And how could she battle with the world? Why, she looked like some petted heiress —she looked every inch the nobly born aristocrat— and what was she?

"With her origin, with her history," thought the old housekeeper, "her life should be quiet and obscure. But she is a brilliant young creature, and her mother's fate is likely to be hers also. Why didn't she die r Mrs. Quillet's silence and grim dissatisfied manner I did not trouble Gwendoline. n the pleasure and excitement of her drive, she scarcely noticed them. They arrived at the station in good time. The boat was in waiting when they reached Calais, and they went aboard at once. Mrs. Quillet was ill upon the voyage and retreated to the cabin, while Gwendoline, who was not ill, sat Upon the deck, regarding the crowd of people, the water, and the chalk cliffs of Dover with a strange delight. "I seem to be just beginning life," she thought, with the enthusiasm of seventeen. "The world

.. -- looks all new to me to-day." • No incident mare ed the voyage, and they landed at Dover end took the train for London, where they stopped at a small family hotel. Here the old housekeeper procured a private sitting-room and two bedrooms. "We've brought her up as a lady," the good dame muttered -to herself, "and now we'll have to treat her as a lady. I could no more ask the child to share my bedroom than I could fly. And as to taking her down to the coffee room to eat, with all those mercantile people to stare at her, that is- impossible "' So dinner was eaten in the private sitting-room. Mrs. Quill et hesitated about taking her place at the table, with an uncomfortable dread of taking liberties with this high-bred, aristocratic young lady, but Gwendoline would not sit down until she had done so, and she was forced to yield. " Yon are as wilful as ever," said the old house- keeper, with a faint smile. " You will rule us all at Lonernoor, I foresee." The name "Princess," by which Gwendoline had been known in her earliest years had clung to her ever since. The name fitted her well. Her nature was royally graud and noble—her beauty royally splendid. When the dinner things had been removed, the girl approached the housekeeper with something of yearning in her face. She longed to kiss her—to tell her that she was glad to see her, to express something of the affection that filled her heart to- wards this nurse and guardian of her earliest years, but Mrs. Quillet's countenance was cold and for- bidding, and Gwendoline siyhed, walked on co the window, and then sat down again. It was impossible, even to our warm-hearted, im- pulsive young heroine, to utter words of affection to one so frigid as Mrs Quillet now chose to be. "Are you not satisfied with me, Mrs. Quillet ?" said Gwendoline, after a little silence. "Satisfied !" repeated the old woman' with a start. "Why should I not be satisfied? You are tb lady, Miss Gwendoline. It is not for me to be dissatisfied with you." "I shall be glad to see dear old Lonemoor again, , said Gwendoline thoughtfully. "Is my guardian at home now ?" "Your—what 1" "My guardian—Squire Markham, you know, How odd that I should never in my life have seen him. Is he at Lonemoor ?" "Squire Markham is still abroad," replied the housekeeper, dryly. iillhi‘ "Strange he has been abroad so many years," said Gwen, meditatively. "How I long to see him. I hope he'll come home soon. I mean to devote myself to him. Nurse Quillet. I mean to be a loving daughter to him. Be shall never regret the expense he has lavished upon me." The hou,ekef3per smiled oddly, but a displeased expression showed itself in her eyes. "If you can win the squire's liking, Miss Gwendo. line," she said, " you'll do a fine thing for yourself. tries." "If he never comes back, Mrs. Quillet, what is to become of me ?" asked the girl, gravely. " Heaven knows, and it alone !" the old womar groanennvoluntarily. " What do you mean ?" "Nothing. "And so I am to live in seclusion," said Gwenclo• line, sighingly. " I've had such delightful dreamt of new acquaintances, Visits, pleasures—but nevei mind. I shall be contented, Nursey. And my guardian will come home soon ; I am sure of it. We'll look on the in ight side. I shall find some. thing to do at Lonemoor, and I shall not be ' lonely." After a little further conversation, the young girl said good night and retired to her own room. Long after her bright head had pressed the pillow tr sleep, the old housekeeper sat in the little parlour, face to face with the great problem of Gwendoline'e future. She had brought up the girl as a lady. Now whal was she to do with her?

"i cola n,.t do otherwise than eaucate ner," shE thought," even though the master would do nothing for her, and has never even noti6ed her existence, and though he has never allowed one penny for her support and education. Whether John and I were wise ir educating tier as we have done we shall find out, f suppose. Meantime, she is not suited for a gover• ness, even if she could get a situation, with that face of hers. It would never do to put her to the millinery or dressmaking. No, she must go tc Lonemoor. But in what capacity ?" How strange it seemed to her that she had neve' considered these questions before. "The bailiff's family vi ill think it very odd thn,t, I should keep her at Lonemoor as a young lady., she said to herself uneasily. "But they have ne power to send her away, even if they don't like het presence there. People may look askance at hen The rector wrote a nice letter to Madame de Loraine about her three years ago, but he would not bring his daughters to visit her. No one will look kindly upon her. She does not suspect one syllable con' cerning her origin.. How long can she remain ignorant of what people whisper among themselves concerning her vagrant motherl. If I had MUT place to se.iti tier to, id never send her to Lonemom —never! But she must go there, and I must watch over her." With this pious resolve the housekeeper werrt lied, but she could not sleep. She wished now that she had educated the child for a governess, and oot allowed her to return to Lox:moor. THE OPENING.

Of Second session of the thirteenth Parliament ut the Queen, and of the twenty-fifth Parliament Of the United Kingdom of Great Britain and Ire- was opened on Tuesday by Ko3 al Commis- sion, in the House of Lords. The attractions of the e-honoured opening ceremony were enhanced by „To new decorations and upholstery of the chamber. she benches had been freshly • covered with red leather, and in place of the heavy oak pendants Which formerly carried the cup lights, there !Mug from the roof ten light brass branches, carry-- lug the electric light. The ladies attended in taore than ordinary numbers. The four baok benches on the Opposition side were given up to them, and were fully occupied, and many were seated on the Ministerial side. Ladies as well -as gentlemen were, as is usual on these occasions, admitted to the strangers' gallery, and the side galleries had many occupants of both sexes. There Were about fovty peers in attendance. Shortly after two o'clock the Lord Chancellor entered from the bar and took his seat on the woolsack, but im- rcediately left the House, passing out by the side of the throne. In a few minutes the noble and learned lord returned, accompanied by Earl Spencer, the Earl of lirnherley, the Marquis of Breadalbane, and Lord Car- rington' all, as Commissioners, wearing their full peers' tobes of scarlet and ermine. The Commissioners being seated on a bench specially placed for them in front of the throne, Colonel Butler, the Yeoman Usher of the slack IRod, -was directed by the, Lord Chancellor to summon the Commons. After a short interval, the ,Speaker, attended by the Serjeant-at-Arms, and fol- tolled by a considerable number of members ,of the Lower House, attended at the bar, and the Lord 'Chan- cellor having stated that her Majesty had directed a Commission under the -Great Seal to be issued for the 9)ening of Parliament, directed the Hon. ,Slingsby zethell, the reading clerk,to read the Commission. ' THE QUEEN'S SPEECH. The Lord Chancellor said :—My Lords and Gentle- Inen,—We are commanded to read to you her Majesty's alvech,in her Majesty's own words. :He then proceeded ‘0 read the speech as follows :

My LO-HDBAND GENTIRMEN,- SAPEOPEAN PEACB.

I continue to 4old friendly and harmonious relations With ail Foreigil Powers. Their declarations in every quarter are favourable to the maintenance of European 'Peace.

UGANDA.

„In connection with the approaching evacuation of uganda by the British East Africa Company, I have deemed it expedient to authorize a Commissioner of experience and ability to examine on the spot, with adequate provisions for his safety, into the best means of dealing with the country, and to report to my Govern- ment upon the subject.

THE LABOUR COMMISSION.

Sir T. H. Farrel', ex-permanent secretary of the Board of Trade and Alderman of the London County Council, yesterday week,at the request of the.Labour Commission, appeared before that body to give evidence with refer- ence to the action of the London County Council on labour questions. Sir Thomas recited the resolutions of the Council in relation to the increase of wages, the shortening of hours, and payment for overtime, and relief as far as possible from Sunday duty ,of the park- keepers, gardeners, and other ,employss of the council which had resulted in an increased cost to the rate- payers of £B,OOO, while the increase of wages paid to old ' pensioners and others in charge of open spaces taken ' over from Lord Meath's Metropolitan ,Gardens Asso- ciation had raised the cost of , the eharges of those places from £9OO to £1,40.0 a year. Be criticized the policy ef the Council in taking action in this manner without having full facts Were them, and said that it appeared that it was based won the idea that 245. a meek was the minimum wage that should be paid to adult workers in London„ without reference to the ,quality, nature, or value of their work, or the capacity of the workers; but he pointed aut that. at .Kew the men were paid 18s. a week, and ,any number of good men could begot at that rate, while,Ailae Council paid 245. and 275. a week ; and even the concessions that had been made had not fully satisfied those employed by the Council. If payment were made to the , Govern- ment servants employed in the samemapacity at the same rate it would incaease the cost of management of the central parks from .5..a0,p00 to z 26,000 a year. Thelabour in the parks was but a. fraction of the whole labour employed by the Council, and theiquestion ought . be considered as a whole, and not in reference to one department only. The ambition of the Council was to get transferred to it the water ,and gasandertakings, 8,1E4 to obtain control of the tramways ; but if in ac- ceptiqg the transfer. the Council increased the labour big to the same extent as in the ,ease of .the parks it would,soon outrun any ecomomy that might be effected. In fact, the action of the Council in that matter had led many to doubt the wisdom of .tacquiring those undertakings, and in regard to the water supply bad comiderahly damped his own ardonr, so that, he was almost inclined to think, with Sir .johaLebbock, that the NI .ater should remain in the hands of the:companies. There was a disposition in the Council to. underpay brain woe: and to overpay musacle work. The policy of the Ccemait is imposing conditions ,as to wages upon contractors had led to very large ,increased expense to the ratepayers, and necessitated the tender for Barking- road Bridge being increased by „Es,sra,.which increase the Council, accepted. If that was to be the result of the Council's action it was time that they had done With contracts, but the institution of municipal svorkshops,ramless they were managed with care ' roresig,ht, and economy, would prove a great evil. If they were to be run in the intenests,of the workmen ~employed instead of the interests of the xatepayerc they viwauld add to the cost of the work very many times the expense saved in contractors' profits.. There was; but one fund ott of which labour could be paid, and that fund was not inexhaustible. so so .that any excessive demand upon it by any , one class of workmea must diminish the share which others would get, eitbemby making the produce of their own labour dearer, .and thus lowering real wages, or !?37 11 exhausting the fund and thus lowering money wages as well. Thelact that they could not make amake larger by dividing it, noreould they divide a shilling amongst twelve men so that each man should receive twopence. Be would-be gladto.see labour mem the largest share of the net profit of industry which it could receive with- out cutting its own throat or without killing the goose that laid the goldeaeggs. Under municipal ecriplociment the great evil to be feared was that men would not rise to their best level, as well as that it would lead tozCarge increase of expense.

WARRINGTON SUNDAY SCHOOL TEACHERS ASSOCIATION.

QUARTERLY MEETING.

The quarterly meeting of the above-named asso- ciation was held in the Museum-street Schools, on Monday. Tea was provided for the large number of Sunday School teachers who had gathered to- gether from all parts of the town, and afterwards a meeting was held, presided over by Rev. E. C. E. Carleton.—The Chairman said he was very glad to welcome the association there that night, because he felt the great value of an organization of that kind in connection with their Church Sunday Schools. Be was quite sure it could be made the means of doing very excellent work, and it was very good for them when engaged in any common wcrk to meet together from time to time for mutual counsel and en- couragement. There were times when united action had and ought to be taken by the Sunday School teach- ' ers of the town, and that could be best done through an agency of that kind. Of course the most important work that they had to consider with respect to the association was as to how they might learn to do their work as Sunday school teachers better. Certainly there was one way, and that was by making them take more interest in it. He believed it was the wish of I many interested in the organisation that they should have more lessons, and he was glad to be able to tell them that that would be carried out at the next meet- ing. (Applause.)—The Rev. R. C. Hodgins, of Liver- pool,`read a paper on "The work of the Sunday school in relation to the daily life of the young." He was of opinion that the work of the Sunday school teacher ex- tended beyond the Sunday school and the Sunday. He also firmly believed that one reason why Sunday school teaching failed to produce much good effect in the lives of those taught was the absence of dogma in the lessons of many teachers. They had heard of " jelly-fish religion,', and there was also a great deal of " jelly-fish teaching. A vote of thanks to Mr. Fiodgins for his excellent paper, on the motion of Mr. Jolley, concluded the pro- ceedings.

WESTON POINT READING ROOIL

• SPEECH BY MR. BRUNNER M.P. The opening ceremony in connection with the Weston Point Reading Room and Coffee Tavern was performed by Mr. J. T. Brunner M.P.. in the presence of a good assemblage. Mr. Brunner was accompanied by Mrs. Brunner. Proceedings com- menced with a tea, of which about 50 persons par- took, the tables being presided over by Mrs. R. Clark. Mrs. F. W. Clarke, Miss Kate Rosenberg, Mrs. Rees, Mrs. Hamersley, and Mrs. Frith. Afterwards the Vicar. the Rev. J. H.Wilcockson, took the chair. The Chairman gave a short history of the reading room movement. There had been a reading room in the house on the Pierhaad, now occupied by Mr. Jones, but it had died a natural death shortly after he came to Weston Point. Matters were allowed to drift for some time, hut when the place began to get filled with men working on the Ship Canal, many of them young men, it began to be perfectly clear to his mind that some- thing must be done for the social w.ll-being of these young men. They had only two alternatives before them ; they must either go to the public-house, which was not good for them, or they might go into the lanes, which was not good for them either. A meeting of the inhabitants was held to consider the question, and a committee was appointed, with the result that they had obtained a coffee tavern and reading room. The Weaver Trustees had kindly allowed them a site just outside the school railings.at the nominal rental of 2s 6d. ,per annum. Then the hon. Rev. Grunston, of the Ship Canal, came to their assistance as to the building. He ' promised to pay half the cost of the removal of the mission room from Ellesmere Port, to Weston Point on condition that the Navvies' Mission was allowed to hold a service in it on Sunday evenings. Altogether the Committee had spent £2B for the unassuming building, and it was not theirs. But the Ship Canal Company had pledged their word to allow it to remain there for ' of least 18 months, and they had offered to sell it for £34 10s. That sum was out of their reach. Up to the present, they had a guarantee fund which had been subscribed by gentlemen in Weston Point, to the amount of £lB, and one subscription of a guinea. Anyone over 16 years of age could join the reading room,and the coffee tavern was open to the public generally. It ap- peared to him that the establishment ought to be self supporting, and it ought to be managed by those for whose benefit it existed. They had now between fifty and sixty members. They intended to have a village library in connection with the reading-room; and he had already got a nucleus in s couple of volumes presented by a good friend. It was a pleasure to find himself on the same platform with Mr. Brunner, and he hoped it would not be the last time. (Applause.) It was well known that they did not hold exactly the same opinion upon every conceivable point, but it was a very great thing when they could find one plank in a platform upon which they could stand with a man to whom they were opposed in matters by no means unessential and hold out a han3 and call him "brother?' It was much better that they should endeavour net to find points where they differed, but points where they agreed and could work together. (Applause.) Mr. Brunner, who was well .received, said upon the subject they had in their minds that night, Mr. Wilcockson and he were very •thoroughly agreed. What did the possession of a reading,room in a place like Weston Point mean ? In hiscpinion it meant an opnor- tunity for everybody of pleasant, cheerful, and intellec- tual society for every evening of the • year. He wished that thepoorest amongst them should have just as free and good opportunity for intellectual, pleasant, and cheerful society as the richest amongst them. (Ap- plause.) Therefore it was that he came there that night with very great pleasure. He was glad to hear that the reading-room was to be the home of a library; and he advised them to send a subscription to Runcorn Free Library, and get Mr. Jones, the librarian, te send them down books and constantly change them. (Hear, hear.) The subscribers to the reading-room ought to have, and he beliered according to the rules, had the free and unfettered control of the management of it. That was a manly rule, but they must remember that with freedom came responsibility, and every member of the club ought to feel himself • bound to make it go, and to see that it was well managed. If the club put a piece of work into a member's hands let him set himself like a man to do that work as if it were his own, and not only for • others. (Hear, hear.) If next time he came to WestemPoint, he heard that the Club was being managed in that -spirit, he should rem- ember that evening, and he would congratulate himself that there was in Weston 'Point that spirit of manliness which not only rejoiced in fee dom but bore cheerfully and manfully the responsibilities of freedom. (Ap- plause.) He had made op his mindthat for every pound subscribed to the club he would subscribe another. (Ap- plause.) Now let them see whether they could not get the debt cleared off and the place, bought. (Applause.) He would also send an instalment towards the Library. (Applause.) There was one ,particular aspect of educa- tion which had been very greatly neglected amongst them ; he meant the inculeating.of a knowledge of how to keep ourselves in good hwathv and he proposed, sub- ject to the approval of the Committee, to send, to begin with, a number of books upon this subject, which was described by scientific men as 1 physiology. For the ladies he would send more than one book, but one particular book on the management of infancy. There was nothing more necessary ' for grown up people to know than how a baby ought to be managed in order to keep its health. And he would MI them why. Because the number of babies who died under twelve months was something absolutely appalling. The Bishop of Peterborough on Friday last at Liverpool, said educa- tion ought to be directed towards the development of character. But no matter how highly developed the character of any mother might be, let her be as full of that charity so beautifully described in the Bible, as she eould be, nevertheless if she did not' know how to treat her baby, if she ignorantly fed•it,• the baby would die, and the mother's life would be broken. They ought to teach each other how to keep their 'children alive and in health, and they ought to teach the girls in the whole Country, because girls had the management of babies at a very early age, and they ought to teach each other , how tornaintain their own lives .in comfort. Therefore, always-endeavouring as lac did to begin at the beginning, he would send a number of bodks on physiology, and if the Consraittee said they wanted sortie more books, they had got nothing to do but Mk for them. (Ap- plause.) Mr. Brunner thenleft the room, accompanied by the lentire audience, and opened the door of the Coffee I Tavern and Reading Room. In tire latter room, Mr. Brunwer delivered a brief address. Ile was glad bulked, he said, toqsay a first visit to Weston Point Reading Room. He hoped it was the beginniug,of an institution which would last for their day, and though not perhaps in the shape in which it presented itself to their eyes navw, for their children .and those who came after -them. There was not as a rule mueli prospect of permanence with an institution of this kind in the hands.of a Voluntary Committee. In his time he had seen mgreat many such institutions started anti he had neverknown one last for very many years, and he therefore -recommended them to put it upon a per- manent foundation. There were two ways in which this oauld be done. They could adopt the Free Libraries Act (hear hear) .and put themselves under the kind -rotecting wing .cf his friend Mr. -Tones, who sat on his ght. Or if they felt strong enough to do it, they could ,a,dopt the Act for themselves, and that particular Act which was paused at the instance of Sir 50hn Lubbock a short time ago specially to fit the law relating to libraries oi.',auch cases as theirs at Weston Point. He recom-

FATAL ACCIDENT ON THE RAILWAY

AT ORFORD. A FOREMAN CUT TO PIECES.

THE VIEWING OF MUTILATED BODIES:

A shocking accident occurred on the Cheshire Lines Railway, opposite Orford Hall, on Saturday morning, when Edward Appleton, a foreman plate- layer, was knocked down about 7.30 by a light engine. Deceased had evidently been walking between the rails of the four foot, for when his body was found he was lying full length within the rails, terribly mutilated. The head was crushed to a pulp and beyond recognition. His left arm and shoulder had beeji torn off, and both his legs were broken. The right arm and leg had both been cut off, and were lying two feet from the metals. The upper portion of the body was terribly @rushed. The body must have been carried about 20 yards by the engine, as portions of the deceased's brains were found about that distance from where the body was, One foot could not be found, and it is presumed that it must have been carried away by the engine. 'the police were soon on the spot, and the portions of the body collected together and carried away on the ambulance. Deceased was about 33 years of age, was married, and resided in Hale-street, Warrington. Mr. F. A. Jones. deputy coroner, held an inquest at the Horse and Jockey Inn' Winwick-road, on Monday evening,on the body of the deceased. Atr.Simpson,super- intendent of the Cheshire Lines Cnmmittee, was present After the jury had been sworn the foreman said the jury wanted to know whether it was absolutely necessary to view the body, which had been "crewed down" since Saturday. The Coronet said it was absolutely necessary. to do so, because otherwise he could not give a certifieate. A Juror : It is very wrong' think that considering the "dilapidated" atste of the body is in it is not fit to be

seen. The Coroner : I ant bound to administer the law as it is. I should have been glad to have acceded to your re- guest if it had been in my power, but I am afraid that you will have to satisfy the law. rhe jury then went to view the body, and while they were doing so the Coroner proceeded to examine the wit- nesses. Lucy Appleton said the deceased was her husband ,and he had been living at 19, Hale-st., off Longford-st. He was a foreman platelayer for the Cheshire Lines Com- mittee. On Saturday morning he left home to go to his work a few minutes after seven o'clock, when he was in his usual health. His hearing and sight were good. Be was brought home dead about eight o'clock. Witness also informed the coroner that the people who brought the coffin screwed the lid on. Robert Jaggard, an engine driver on the Midland Railway, said that on Saturday morning he was the driver of a train from Derby, and he approached Orford about 7.30. Be observed something lying on the up line near Orford Hall, and as quickly as possible he stopped his train, and then went back to see what the" something" was. He found that it was a plate- layer who was dead. Part of his head was cut off, and one hand lay in the down road. He gave information to some platelayers, and the signalman at Winwick-road went to the scene of the accident. At this stage the jury returned to the room and then the Coroner said he had inquired into the matter. It I appeared that the coffin lid was screwed down by the leoffm-maker, who had no right to act in such a manner I before the inquest. He ought to have known that it was necessary for the jury to viva the body. It must I have been a most unpleasant thing to view the body, and it was a pity the matter did not lie in the Coroner's discretion. The Foreman .(interposing) : The ijury thought yem would have had power to use your discretion, thetbody having been screwed up. The Coroner If you think that a coroner is a proper person to be invested with discretion there are some members of the press present and no doubt they will take notice of it. James Turner, an engine driver on the Cheshire Lines Railway, and residing at Stockport, said he was the driver of a down goods train from London on Saturday morning, and arrived near Orford Hall about 7.20. Be noticed dneased with his hammer on his shoulder going towards Padgate. At the same time he saw the head light of an engine •coming towards him. Deceased stood in the four foot with his back towards the approaching engine. Witness shouted "Mind this en- gime," and deceased must have heard him' for he turned round just as the engine was 30 or -4:0 yards away from him. Witness looked back shortly afterwards, but could see nothing, as it was rather dark, and the steam from the engine obscured the view. Deceased had ample time to gat out of the way. Arthur 'Luther, engine driver on the Manchester, Sheffield, and Lincolnshire Railway, gave evidence, and MrSimpson produced the rules of thecomPany,by which platelayers are required to step off the railway altogether when they see a train approaching. A verdict of" Accidental death" was returned, and a rider was added to the effect that the viewing of bodies should be left in the discretion of the Coroner. A vote of sympathy with the widow of deceased was unani- mously passed by the jury.

• CHOLERA PRECIUTIONS, LIVERPOOL, RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

The Government inquiry into the arrangements of the port sanitary authorities of Liverpool and Runcorn was resumed on Monday at the Liverpool Town Hall by Mr. Jenner-Fust and Dr. Barry, two inspectors under the Local Government Board. The various riparian authorities on the Mersey were , again represented. Mr. Jenner-Fust explained what was done on the last occasion. Since then the Health Committee had passed a reselution that, assuming they remained the port sani- tary authority, an assistant medical officer and one or more port sanitary inspectors should be appointed upon the condition specified in the Medical Officer's Report, and that suitable offices should be erected at the Pier- head. The Wallasey Local Board had also passed a resolution recommending that the port sanitary autho- rity be constituted by the formation of a joint board, on which the Wallasey board should have two or more re- presentatives; further that in the event of the Liverpool City Council being constituted the port sanitary autho- rity under an improved system of ship visitation, the Wallasey board claimed to appoint two of their body on it. Such a recommendation from so important an authority was entitled to every consideration. The adjournment had enabled the inspectors to report that there was practically a unanimous feeling against the ports of Liverpool and Runcorn being joined together and having one board, as it would require new machin- ery. They recommended that the Liverpool City Council, through its Health Committee, should remain the port sanitary authority for the customs port of Liverpool. The assurance had been given that the com- mittee were not aware of the inefficiency of their pre- sent arrangements, and were prepared to put them in an efficient condition. The Local Government Board agreed in the views laid before them, and had printed a draft order which would assist the various authorities in their deliberations that day. So far as the port of Runcorn was concerned, the Local Government Board agreed that it was impossible to leave the present in- dividual sanitary authorities to act each for themselves, and that it would be better to form a port sanitary authority for Runcßrn, and to allow an arrangement to be made between Runcorn and Liverpool in dealing with cholera cases. This would get over the difficulty of having no mooring station in the Runcorn port for dealing with cholera-infected ships. As the Ship Canal developed, these circumstances would change, and no doutt whatever order was made would have to be considered later on. The draft order of the Local Government Board was read and dealt with. It constituted the City Council the port sanitary authority for the Customs port of Liverpool, and recited the limits of jurisdiction, the assignment of powers under various Acts of Parliament, the payment of expenses and accounts. It was stated that the inspectors proposed that the various riparian autho- rities should contribute towards the expenses of the port sanitary authority in the following proportions : Liverpool, 7260; Birkenhead, 1120; Bootle, 630 ; Bromborough, 0.25 ; Garson, 180; Lower Bebington, 050; Toxteth Park, 3.30 ; and Wallasey. 4.os.—Captain Heron said that if Wallasey was not represented on the Board it would not contribute, as rating must depend upon representation. The case of Runcorn was next taken. Mr. Lightburn said he was glad the Commissioners were prepared to allow Runcorn to continue the arrange- ment with Liverpool as to cholera cases. They would like, if it had been possible, that Liverpool should deal with all cases. • Mr. Oppenheim, town clerk of Widnes, said his borough did not wish to be included in any other author- ity, and he produced a resolution of the Town Council against being included in the Runcorn Port Sanitary Authority, because no foreign vessels came to Widnes. Mr. Jenner-Fust said their opinion was that there should be either no port sanitary authority for Runcorn or that there should be one which would embrace the whole of the Customs port. Mr. Lightburn said that vessels might come to Widnes from home infected ports. Mr. Oppenheim said that in that case his authority was prepared to deal with them. The inspectors expressed a hope that as Widnes only objected, that authority would come to some arrange- ment. They would adjourn the inquiry to a convenient day at Runcorn. Dr. Bligh asked what provision would be made as regarded coasting vessels from infected ports. Dr. Barry said it would be the duty of the port sani- tary authority to inspect them from time to time, and if any infectious disease arose they would take the same action as with vessels from infected ports. So far as Liverpool was concerned they hoped that more than one inspector would be appointed, in addition to an assis- tant medical officer. The inquiry was then adjourned to Runcorn, at two p.m., on February 16. •••

WA.RRINGTON AMATEUR PHOTO- GRAPHIC SOCIETY.

i 10 44146.

The sixth annual general meeting of this society was held in the committee-room of the Museum on Tuesday evening. Mr. Thomas Welsby presided, and there was a fair attendance. Five new mem- bers, the Rev. E. T. Reed, Mr. Gilbert Harding, Mr. Harry Milling, Mr. Hill, and Mr. Green having been elected ,the report of the last annual meeting was adopted. The secretary, Mr. Pritchard, presented his report for the past year, which stated that the condition of the society was satisfactory in all respects. 21. new members had been admitted during the year ; two had resigned. The total number of members now was 78. Mention was made of the various papers that had been read at monthly meetings, the exhibition, and the" rambles' during the summer months. The report was adopted, and Mr. Pritchard heartily thanked for his services. Mr. P. Dalton bon. treasurer, read his financial state- ment,which showed a balance in favour of the society of ss. 10d. The Chairman, Mr. J. Hallows, and others, spoke in warm terms of the attention and time Mr. Dalton had given in the interests of the association, and a vote of thanks was accorded him by acclamation.-15 members were elected by ballot to form the Council, the secretary, Mr. Kirby, and Mr. Dalton being nomi- nated tellers, and the following gentlemen were elected : —Messrs. C. Aylward, P. Dalton, T. J. Down, S. Fair- hurst, J. Hallows, .5. Harding, (Eustace-street), J. Hard- ing, (Liverpool-roadl, John Hesketh, H. N. Houghton, G. Kirby, W. Pierpoint, A. Paul, T. Weisby, J. Skelton, and H. Pritchard. A vote of thanks to the Chairman closed the business. The Council met immediately after for the appointment of officers. Mr. W. J. Down re-elected president, and Mr. H. N. Efoughton and Mr. W. Pierpoint vice-presidents. Mr. H. 'Pritchard, at the hearty solicitation of the Council, again, accepted the secretaryship ; and Mr. P. Dalton was elected treasurer. Mr. Skelton retains his post as librarian 1 Mr. .J. Fair- hurst was re-appointed auditor. The question of a cus- todian of the society's property—which amounts, allow- ing for depreciation, to nearly £so—was mooted. Mr. Dalton has hitherto had charge of this, but the rapid growth of the society causing the applications for lanterns, cameras, &c.., to increase, entails upon him so serious a loss of tiine, besides interruption to his business, that he is compelled to refuse the trust any longer. The touncil decided to meet at an early date to consider an amendment of the rules bearing upon the question, with a view to report to a special meeting of the

society.
