"ODE TO A PRA.TO PIE."

Bobby Burns hez immortalized Haggis, At least so aw Scotchmen ware;s -- An' ther's nowt the whole world o' cookin', That wi' it con even compare. To this singular far-fotch'd opinion, They're heartily welcoms, say I; But it only proves this, thay'n ne'er tasted A Lancashire grand Prato Pie. Why, a Haggis yo'd tak' for a footbaw, That's stuff'd up wi' onions an' meyl ; An' to make um appear full an' solid, Look cromm'd wi' a fist er brush steyl. But when" Sandy "gets into this country, O'er Haggis he heyves mony a sigh ; An' he never agen feels contentet, Onless he con get emit° Pie. The German loves Seaurkreaut an' Sossige, An' thinks ther's nowt else fit to eyt ; But it only shows heaw tastes 'all differ,— To uz it looks very quare meyt. Ez soon ez he migrates o'er here, His grub poos his face aw awry; An' wen he gets worn, his wole cry is : " Mein Gott ! I vill have Prato Pie !" A Frenchman con feed on owd horses, An' relishes fricasseed frogs ; An' even, if hardly put to it, Con make a good dinner on dogs! But wen he cums here to a banquet, An 'gies ev'ry dish a fair try; He guz welly off it, exclaimin, "acre bleu! donnez moi Prato Pie!" The Welshman rejices in mutton, An' likes it tucht up wi' a leek; But a mon that sits down feelin' hungry, Will eyt a sheep to his own cheek ! Yet ther taste is moor that ov a Christian; But still it 'ull pall by-an'-bye, If thay'n luck to sit deawn neaw an' then to A Lancashire grand Prato Pie I When a Nor'-Easter wind's keenly blowin', An' its bite yo con hardly abide; An' wen o'er yore vvark yo mun face it, Wi' hardly owt i' yore inside •, Then, Men is the time yo feel thankful ; An' turnin' yore e'en up to th' sky, Yo breaust eawt a leaud Alleloolyo, For a Lancashire hot Prato Pie! By gyow, lads! aw th' world o' cookin' There's nowt con cum up to this dish ; An' if yo shud ever invite me, Be sure that yo gie me my wish ! It's woth Grace before it an' after, Ez lung ez a pidgin con fly; ' An' aw'll live an' aw'll dee quite contentet, If yole feed me on good Prato Pie! TUM 0' PEGS.

Rose Bonk, Feb. 4th, 1893.

THE MOULDERS' STRIKE AT ST. HELENS. On Saturday night, a delegates' meeting of moulders was held at the George Hotel, St. Helens, at which the moulders of Warrington, Liverpool, , Birkenhead, Widnes, Wrexham, Chester, and Dublin I were represented. It was unanimously decided that the Executive Committee be instructed to put a levy of 6d. per week on all working members for one week, which mean a sum of abcut .£3OO. In- dependent of this, the districts named, in addition to other districts, are subscribing 6d., and in some cases is. per week, and these subscriptions are being distributed among the men in addition to the usual strike pay of 15s. per week taken from the funds of the society. The St. Helens moulders' chief standpoint in resisting the reduction is the amount of work that has been done during the last twelve months. The trade has been in a far better condition at St. Helens than in any neighbouring town, and, with the exception of Messrs. Daglish's works, where at the end of the year trade was rather slack, no foundry has been stopped a day during the twelve months for want of work. It is stated that at present about fifteen or twenty non- society moulders are working in St. Helens. Six moulders who were working last week, it is said, left Messrs. Varley's on Saturday, and five more were employed on Monday morning.

At the Fleece Hotel, St. Helens, on Tuesday night, the ironfounders of St. Helens, met a deputation Prom the Moulders' Society, to try to come to terms regarding the moulders' strike. After considerable discussion, the meeting lasting three hours, the masters agreed to give the men the old rate of wages for three months, at the end of which, should trade warrant a reduction, the de- putation promised to take it into favourable considera- tion. The men agreed to start at tne foundaries where non-society men have not been employed on Thursday morning, and at the other foundaries when the non- society men have finished work.

===== 0002601_18930204_art0034.txt | included=true | included =====
THE HOMESTEAD STRUGGLE.

MR. CARNEGIE MAKES A STATEMENT.

Mr. Carnegie, of Homestead, had repeatediy been requested to make a statement regarding the recent labour troubles at that place, but always refused until Monday, when, in an interview, he said :—" I came to Pittsburg not to rake up, but to try and bury the past. That is beyond recall, and should be banished as a hor- rid dream, only the lessons it teaches being laid to heart for the future. For 26 years our concerns have run with only one labour stoppage, and I trust axcl believe that this record will be fully equalled in the twenty-five yrars to come. When employer and employed are antagonistic, each considering the other his enemy, it is a contest between twin brothers. There is no genuine victory possible for either, but the defeat of both is the only result." Regarding his own share in the great works bearing his name, Mr. Carnegie said: " I retired from active busi- ness four years ago. It is a great error on the part of our countrymen to endure the cares of business until they break down in harness, at a time when they should be in their prime. I believe in retiring from work betimes and giving younger men a chance. I sold portions of my interests, and am gradually selling more to such young men in the service as partners who are found to be possessed of exceptional ability. lam not an officer of the company, but only a shareholder, with- out power to give instructions to anybody." Mr. Carnegie added that he had the utmost confidence in the young partners.

SAD SUICIDE AT RUNCORN.

PATHETIC LETTER.

------- - A shocking suicide occurred at Runcorn, on Mon- day. On arriving at his home, 46, Bridge-st. about six o'clock on Monday evening, Charles driffiths found his two young children crying in the yard, and exclaiming" Oh, my mamma! " He then saw his wife suspended by a rope from a nail in the 'wall of a passage leading into the yard. He im- mediately fetched P.C. Juett, who is a neighbour, and that officer at once cut down the woman. She was, however, quite dead. She had apparently stood on a chair and then kicked it from under her. The deceased was only 26 years of age, and was the mother of two children, aged respectively six dna three years.—Mr.Worsley, coroner, held the in- quest on Wednesday. The husband said the de- ceased had complained terribly of her head lately, and she had talked very funnily to him. She had never threatened to take away her life. He found the following letter on the mantlepiece in the kitchen.—" Dear Charley,—Tell father to put me in a grave. You have been a good husband to me: I can- not live any longer, take it as light as you can: I know it will be hard to bear. I should be a burden to you ; be good to Charley, and you, my darling husband, keep all my things. I am sorry for you. I know you have nothing but mother has me in a club; she will help you. I will meet you all in that land above. Good-bye to all. Good-bye, good- bye my loving husband. It will upset father and mother. Tell them to bear it as well as they can, lam out of my mind." Witness said he had been employed at Mathieson's Works, Widnos, for three years, and he had always given his wife plenty of money. He could not account for his wife taking her life in the slightest. He never thought it necessary to call in a doctor, be- cause she never gave up.—Mrs. Annie Juett, wife of a police constable, said the deceased had complained to her about her head being bad. She was with the deceased shortly after five o'clock, and she appeared to be in her usual condition then.—The Coroner said it was the sad- dest case he had heard in his long experience as coroner. He thought the woman had committed the rash deed through trouble in her head, and there could be no doubt that she was of unsound miad at the time.— Mr. W. H. Rogerson, foreman, said Griffiths was one of his Sunday school scholars, and he was sure he was ex- pressing the feelings of the jury when he said they deeply sympathised with him. The verdict recorded was that the deceased committed suicide whilst tempor-

insane.

At Bolton, on Thursday, two local barbers, named Charles Thompson and Henry. Battersbv, were sum- moned under an old Act of Charles 11. for shming on the Lord's Day. The Act, which is 300 years old,en icts that no worldly labour, works of necessity and charity excepted, should be performed on the Lorils Day. The Act has never been repealed. One of the defendants had been fined before. Both were again fined. KAY'S Courouxn, a demulcent anodyne expectoront for Coughs.and Colds, 94., 130. Of all Chenaists.—Advt.

PRESENTATION TO SERGEANT-

INSTRUCTOR BIRCH.

On Tuesday evening, a presentation was made by the staff-sergeants and sergeants of the Ist Volunteer Battalion Prince of Wales Volunteers (South Lancashire Regiment), to Sergeant-Instruc- tor Birch. After supper, in the recreation room, Colour-Sergeant Potter presiding, and the whole staff being present, the company adjourned to the sergeants mess room, Colour-Sergeant Potter presiding there also. The usual loyal toasts having been drunk, the Chairman read a letter to him from Lieutenant Appleton apologizing for his inability to be present. In the course of this letter the Lieutenant said :—" I always feel that to get a sight of Sergeant Birch's face is like a tonic or a week at the seaside, and there will now be a double dose of the tonic at Halton, which will for the future be known as an exhilarating health resort, where weary Volunteers will make excursions in search of new life and spirits."—The Chairman said they had assembled that evening to make a small presentation to their late Sergeant-Instructor (who had left the corps and was living at Halton), as a token of the esteem in which be was held by them. They had never had the pleasure of meeting one in that room of whom they thought more. highly. (Applause,) They were, of course, very sorry that he had left them, but they trusted he would be, spared for many years, arid that they w?uld occasion-- . ally have the p,asiire of meeting him in th 4 DOI Ball% in which he and the sergeants had spent many pieZvat evenings. (Applause.) An excellent portrait of Mr. Birch, elegantly framed, and enlarged from a recent photograph by Mr. Birtles, of Sankey-street, was then unveiled amidst applause.— The late Quartermaster Heaton then made the presenta- tion. He referred to Mr. Birch's connection with the corps, and to his many good sterling qualities, and said he had not been more than a week with them when he had secured the respect of all with whom he was brought into contact.—Sergeant-Major Fennelly and Sergeant-Instructor Sullivan (Newton Corps) also spoke. —A recitation descriptive of the battle of Majuba Hill was given by Sergeant McCoy, after which Mr. Birch, who was received with hearty cheering, responded. He said although he was doing very well where he was, better even than in Warrington, it was with consider- able regret that his connection with the Warrington Volunteers had been severed, and the picture would serve to remind him of the pleasant hours he had spent amongst them. He felt unable to adequately return thanks for the too flattering remarks which had been made concerning him, but this he would say, that their kindness would never be effaced from his memory. (Applause.)

A water bailiff, named Mallin, at Dungiven, county Derry, has been committed to trial for shooting at an alleged poacher named Murphy when a few yards from him. The bullet entered Murphy's cheek, and lodged close to the car. It was afterwards extracted. The magistrates refused bail.

Considerations such as these should moderate the confidence of those impetuous persons who, speaking from the fulness ef their desires, prognosticate an early termination Of the labours of the new Parliament. It is by nn means nnlikely that the majority which is behind the Government will display a vitality, a unity, and a determination that will discomfit its enemies and meet the most sanguine wishes of its friends. _ _

_ The Queesiii speech which was read by the LORD CRANCELLOR on Tuesday and which, of course, ex- Presses the sentiments of the Ministry in power, Was hrief and lucid, which cannot always be said / these documents. The state of affairs in Egypt was referred to, and it is satisfactory to note that the young and not over discreet Khedive has declared in satisfactory terms that he Will follow henceforward the established Practice of previous consultation with the Ilritizh Government in political affairs. There ia no change of English policy with regard to the period of the occupation of Egypt, but as long as the British Lion is in the Land of the Pharoalis he is determined to be treated with a Proper amount of respect. Of domestic measures, the Home Rule Bill, as was to be expected, comes in for first mention. "It has been prepared," we al'e told, "with the desire to afford contentment to the Irish people, important relief to Parliament, and additional securities for the strength and union of the Empire." Although the Government had undertaken to bring in this Bill on the earli- est available occasion—and indeed Mr. dLADSTONE has intimated that he will move the first reading 1:)n Monday next—yet Mr. BALFOUR, to say oothing of the smaller fry of the Tory party, dis- Played on Tuesday night an indecent haste to criticise a measure of which he does not know the Provisions. Be thereby laid himself open to the attack of Mr. GLADSTONE who sarcastically pointed out that the leader of the opposition had weakened' hic position by showing animus against a measure before he knew what it was. There are many Other reforms enumerated in the QUEEN'S Speech 4Cotne of which when enacted cannot fail to have 151-reaching and beneficial influences. Amongst these are the amendment of the system of regis- tration, the shortening of the duration of Parlia- clent, and the equalising of the franchise by giv- lug to one man one vote. Then there are various hills ,bearing on the condition of labour, the crea- tinn of Parish Councils, the preventioß of the 4towth of new vested interests in the eoelesiasti: cal establishments in Scotland and Wales, and ccliSMlcesi contre,l cyer the Ilan- trffic. Public Bodies 'TkiLondOtt County Council is a thei and , • democratic body, probably the r Eml)l°3"' diAinctly democratic local Inathority that exists, in'England. ~It is, therefore,, rather remarkable tlial Sir T. ===== 0002601_18930204_art0053.txt | included=false | skipped/gap: fewer than 20 non-control characters after cleanup =====
I

===== 0002601_18930204_art0054.txt | included=true | included =====
disposition in the Council to under-pay brain work , and over-pay muscle work. This, it must be ad- mitted, is a complete reversal of the usual order of things. As a rule, there are to be found few more striking contrasts than exist between the large salaries paid to those officials who are sup- posed to do the "brain work "of public bodies and the scanty remuneration of those who do the hard manual drudgery. When we come to look into this not very serious indictment of Sir T. H. FARRER it does not seem to rest upon anything much more substantial than the fact that the London County Council has fixed its minimum wage at twenty-four shillings a week. This, after all, is not an outrageously exorbitant sum for a workman and his family to live upon in the great Metropolis, but then, says Sir T. 11. FARRER, there are men who would undertake to do the sanle work for eighteen shillings a week. So there are; just as when Sir T. 11, FARRER was per- manent secretary of tho Board of Trade there were scores of very capable men who would have been willing to accept that office at a salary less by a few hundreds of pounds a year than was at that time being paid to him. The Board of Trade did wisely in not dealing with its permanent secre- tary, as the ex-permanent secretary now wishes the London Council to deal with its inferior grades of employes. It is not becoming of public bodies to employ the arts and methods of the sweater in dealing with their servants, and they have none of the exeuses for so doing which a private employer may sometimes advance. Sir T.H. Flamm may think he is more enlightened and far-seeing than his colleagues, yet in reality he is in regard to this particular question only a belated economic purist.

PRITCHARD'S have now stood the test for 50 years andquary and first-class workmanship are still guaranteed.—Advt. FATAL FALL DOWNSTAIRS AT WIDNES. On Monday afternoon, an inquest was held before Mr. F. A. Jones. deputy coroner, at the Waterloo Vaults touching the death of a man named Arthur Valley, of 48, Dock-street, who died on Sunday morning, froin injuries received through falling downstairs early that morning. Evidence was given to the effect that the deceased attempted to go downstairs about two o'clock on Sunday morning, when by some means he fell down. He was removed to the sofa, and Dr. Hutchinson sent for, but the unfortunate man did not regain consciousness and died at ten o'clock. The jury returned a verdict of " Accidental death," but were of the opinion that property-owners ought to provide a hand-rail for the stairs in dwelling-houses. A meeting of this society was held on Saturday even- ing under the presidency of Mr. James Johnson. There was a good attendance of officers and members. Mr. Thomas Clarke read an interesting essay on " Woman's Work in Temperance." He spoke of the influence and power that good women possessed, and pointed out how they were taking their proper place' in directing their efforts for the advancement of the people, and removing the evils by which they were surrounded. He referred to the important meetings which were held in Manches- ter during the week, and showed what beneficial results might attend temperance tract distribution, for which the fair sex were so eminently fitted. In the discussion which ensued the following members took part : Messrs. Thomas Cooke, S. M. Timperley, Samuel Henderson, Daniel Bisbrown, John Taylor, Edward Key, and David Smith. A vote of thanks was accorded to Mr. Clarke for his admirable paper, JOSEPH PRITCHARD is prepared to wind and keep in repair, private clocks from 10s. per annum.—Advt. THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, FEBRUARY 4 1893.

RUNCORN FAMOUS FULL BACK. Information reached Runcorn on Thursday that the ,services of Houghton, full back for Runcorn Football Club, would be required for England in the match against Ireland at Dublin on Saturday. Te news created much satisfaction in local football circles. RUNCORN PARISH CHURCH BAND OF HOPE. A meeting in connection with this society was held on Monday evening, under the presidency of the Rev. J. Holden. Songs were interspersed by the adult members, and recitations and dialogues were gone through by the junior members. An interesting address was given by Mr. Hy. Hankinson. THE RUNCORN STABBING CASE. At Runcorn Police Court on Wednesday, the boy Thos. Fisher was again brought up charged with having on the tenth January stabbed Arthur Royle, aged ten years, with a knife.—luspector Cooper said the injured boy was still unable to appear,and the prisoner was remanded for a week. Bail was again allowed.

WIDNIAS FRES LIBRARY. The Free Library Committee have increased the salary of Miss Proctor, the librarian, from £75 to £B5, and the salary of Miss Junes, the assistant, from 15s. to £1 per week. During the last month a daily average of 508 persons had visited the reading room and in the same period 5,184 books were borrowed.

SUCCESS OF A RUNCORN COURSER. Yesterday Mr. John Brundrit, of Runcorn ran several grey hounds at the Ridgway Club (Lytham) coursing meeting, and he succeeded in winning the third prize in the Lytham Cup for 18 all ages, at £3 ss. each, by means of Bessie Raven. His Bessie Hurst also divided the Peel stakes for 19 puppies at £3 ss. each.

RUNCORN GAS COMPANY. The half yearly meeting was held at the GAS Works on Thursday. Mr. F. Rigby, chairman of directors, moved the adoption of the report, which has been published, and the declaration of a dividend at the rate of 10 per cent, per anuum.—Mr. J. Handley, J.P., seconded. Carried. The directors were empowered to borrow on bonds or mortgage the sum of £6,000. - -

WESTON WESLEYAN DAY SCHOOL. A very interesting entertainment was given in this school on Thursday evening in behalf of the Band of Hope. The following ladies and gentlemen took part :—Miss E. Dunbabin and Miss Gilchrist, Messrs. E. H. Worrall, B. Doh, H. Fitton, J. Ball, Coope, Griffiths, Kirkham, Smith, and J. Leathwood. Miss Smith ably accom- panied. Daubs' in chair, No. 122 was the number drawn, entitling Mr. Joseph Booth to an advance of £2OO for a period of 12! years, without interest. This is the third ballot eat has taken place during the last three months, and the next one has been fixed for Wednesday the let March next. SUPPOSED SUICIDE OF A RUNCORN WOMAN. The woman, it appears, left home on Tuesday evening for the purpose of shop- ' ping and she did not return. It is supposed that she committed suicide, having been depressed for SOM9 I time. ACCIDENT TO A WARRINGTON IN OMAN AT RUNCORN. _ .

On Monday morning,shortly before twelve o'clock, a somewhat serious accident s happened to Mrs. Grime,of Warrington.She was on her wayto Runcorn to see her husband, and in descending the steps of the bridge crossing the Mersey, stumbled and fell down them. She was badly bruised and cut about the face and arm. She was conveyed to the surgery of Dr. McDougall, where she received medical at- tention. TrlcarticAL INSTRUCTION AT CROFT.

The first, of a series of lectures was given on Monday night, in the Board School, Croft, by Mr. J. Cawthorne, the schoolmaster, on " Earth, Air, Fire, and Water," illustrated by experiment, iu which he was ably assisted by Mr. Fletcher, of Warrington, assistant master. There was a moder- ate audience, and the lecture, which was a very interesting and appreciative one, was very atten- tively listened to. The various experiments were watched very keenly.

THINGS HEARD AND SEEN AT

ItIINCORN

BY AN OCCASIONAL CORRESPONDENT.

The opening of St. Paul's Parochial Room at Widnes this week was a highly successful function in every point of view. There was a very large company present both in the afternoon and even- ing, and the buPding, which in the interior has somewhat of an old-world .look abaut it, was much admired, both for its convenient arrangements and for its appearance. The building • will be yet another monument to the munificent liberality of Mr. Sutton Timmis, and also ot the Deacon family, to whom, with the addition of Mr. George, educa- tional and religious institutions in Widnes owe much. We echo the wish of Captain Lewis that other manufacturers who have amassed fortunes in the chemical metropolis and removed to localities where the air is purer and, the scenery brighter, could be got at and induced to keep their memories alive by some act of generous 'recognition of the place where glad fortune smiled upon them through clouds of thalr own creating.

Mr. Brunner gave the people of Weston Point, some excellent advice on Friday evening last, on the occasion of the ceremony of opening a coffee tavern and reading room in that village. Among other things he advised his auditors to adopt the Free Libraries Acts. An undertaking of a some- what similar character had been in existence in Weston Point some years ago. but, in the words of the Vicar, the institution had died a natural death. Mr. Brunner added his testimony that such estab- lishments, dependent entirely upon voluntary effort and aid, failed to maintain their position for any lengthened period. Therefore,his advice was to adopt the Libraries Act, whereby the Institution would be given a permanent chairman, and the whole of the ratepayers would contribute their fair share towards the expense and be at liberty to enjoy the advantages and benefits that could be bestowed. In Runcorn the Free Library is one of the most appreciated institutions and the charge its mainten- ance entails upon the rates may be said not to be , felt.

SERIOUS CHARGE AGAINST A RUNCORN TRADESMAN. Yesterday at Runcorn Police Court, Thomas Heesom, pork butcher, Devonshire-square, was brought up on remand from Wednesday, to answer a charge under the Criminal Law Amendment Act. He was accused of attempting to have criminal intimacy with a girl named Annie Geary, under the age ef 16 years, on the 30th January and divers other days. The prosecutrix, whose parents reside at Moore, had been employed by the prisoner as domestic servant for the past three months. —Mr. Lake appeared for the prisoner who was remanded until to-day. He was liberated on Call, himself in £4O and two sureties of £2O each. HOLY TRINITY CHURCH, RUNCORN. We had Co-Operators in cannel' at Euncorn,on Saturday last, They were considering the question " Is co.operation doing its level best tor the poor ?" While there was a consensus of opinion amongst the 60 representatives present that the co-operative societies were doing much for the working-classes, they had the candour to admit that there was room for improvement. Some societies confine their at- tention, in the main, to buying and selling the necessaries of life and dividing the profits in the shape of " divi," while others carry on educational work by means of libraries and reading rooms, and .the teaching by competent men of science and art, and other subjects. It is a regrettable circumstance that the Runcorn Co-operative Society is to he placed in the former class, which it is gratifying to know is much the smaller of the two divisions. Will the members make an effort to rise into a higher plane, and following the example of many societies in Lancashire benefit themselves and the ' people around them by taking an active and prac- tical interest in the educational and social ques- tions of the day? The erection of a Technical In- stitute for the town is delayed for want of funds; the free Library, in consequence of a very smaß income, does not accomplish all the work the com- mittee could wish ' • will the Co-operative Society lend a helping hand by giving or their abundance, and thus confer lasting benefit upon present and future generations of co-operators and others in Runcorn?

RUNCORN BOARD OF COMMISSIONERS.

The monthly meeting was held at the Town Hall, on Thursday morning. Mr. F. Wiswall pre- sided, and there were present Messrs. W. Fisher, P. Speakman, C.C., J. Mea,!owcroft,J. W. Lightburn, T. Lea, S. Taylor, W. liankinson, E. W. Humphreys, D. Bisbrown, J. Dobson, R. Abel, Dr. Mangan, R. Cow- ard, F. A. Lake (law clerk), E. Marshall (secretary), M. Barker (surveyor), Dr. McDougall (medical officer.)

HEALTH OF THE TOWN. The number of deaths registered during the past month amounted to 50. Five deaths were inquest cases and of the remaining 45 deaths, 7 Were due to senile decay, 2 to heart disease, 2 to debility. 9 to bronchitis,3 to measles, 3 to phthisis, 4 to inflammation of the lungs, 2 to mesenteric disease, 2to premature birth. 2 to diseases of the windpipe,and 1 each to viarola,diarrhcea, inflammation of the lining membrane of the brain,disease of glands, erysipelas, peritonitis, chronic disease of the brain, scarlet fever, and diptheria. The 50 deaths show an average death-rate of 30 and,exclusive of the inquest cases, of 27 per 1,000 of the estimated population for the 12 mouths.—The Surveyor said in January last year, there were 48 deaths,of which 4 were inquest cases.—ln rcply to Mr. Lightburn Dr. McDougall said there were not many deaths, from diseases of a preventible charac- ter. Measles was an infantile disease, which sometimes became prevalent at this season of the year, and became very fatal on account of the bronchitis which followed. Ho did not consider the death-rate a very high one. During the past year the death-rate had averaged a fraction over 20 per 1,000.

HIGEMLIS AND BUILDINGS COMMITTEE. This Committee made the following recommendations : —That the sum of £5 be paid to Mr. Streets in respect to his claim for alleged trespass on space in front of house in Church-st., on condition that he executes a convey- ance of the land in question to the Board free from all ' claim to deposit goods thereon ; also that £2 be paid to Mr. and Mrs. G. Boughton on the same terms. That the plan of Mr. T. Reynolds for the erection of 31 houses in a field opposite Greenway Meadows be approved, sub- ject to a section being shown on plan of ashpits and a written description of the mode of construction being given. That the plan of Mr. Isaac Stubbs for the erec- tion of 11 houses in Ivy-road be approved.—Mr. Taylor , moved and Speakman seconded the adoption of the I minutes. Carried.

SIKALLPDX.—BEWARE. Mr. Lightburn, in moving the adoption of the minutes of the Health Committee, said he wished to warn the public through the Press, not to purchase salt fromcarts which perambulate the streets with rags. As an Autho- rity the Commissioners had got no power to stop these carts, and they all knew that rags were a fruitful source for the diffusion of disease. If the public were aware of this fact and acted upon it, it would very much benefit the population. (Hear. hear.)

PROPOSED ELECTRIC LIGHTING. The General Purposes Committee reported that they had received a report from certain members, who had had an informal interview with Mr. Hall, electrical en- gineer, Liverpool, and they had also had before them a letter from that gentleman giving a general and approxi- mate estimate for obtaining an installation of electric light for the public streets, and being of opinion that a case had been made out for further inquiry, they recom- mended that Messrs. A. Hall and Co., electrical engineers Liverpool, be instructed to prepare and present a com- plete and detailed report, specification, and estimate on the whole question, at a cost not to exceed ten guineas. The Committee further recommended that a special com- mittee be appointed to deal with this matter to be called the Electric Lighting Committee, and to consist of Messrs. Speakman, )ileadowcroft, Fisher, Pritchard, flankinson, and Humphreys.—Mr.Hankinsons in moving the adoptien of the minutes said Mr. W. E. Lea had paid a, Visit to Dolgelly, where electric lighting was in operation, and the information he got there was that the electric light superceded gas so far as the price of gas was concerned there, but their machinery was worked by water, and the gas was dearer there than it was at Run- corn. Me, Lea did not think they could hare the electric light in lltmcorn cheaper than gas, but there would be the convenience of it and the advantage to the town. Mr. Lea said he had been approached by two or three shopkeepers as to the probable cost of fitting up the elee- tric light in their establishments, and the Commis.sioners would perhaps be forestalled unless they dealt promptly with the matter,—Mr. Fisher Seconded the motion.—Mr. Humphreys said such a case had been made out for elec- tric lighting that further inquiry and consideration was demanded. Alluding to the successful working of the electric light at Dolg•efly Mr. Humphreys pointed out that the price of gas there was 7s. per 1,000 cubic feet, whereas in Runcoin it was only 3s. 2d., but on the other hand they only paid 30s. per annum per lamp, whereas the Commissioners paid two guineas per lamp. Ile had no hesitation in saying, unless grossly deceived by the gentlemen called in to their aid, that not only could they obtain the electric light for Runcorn without increased cost but at a considerable saving to the ratepayers. The firm al Messrs. Hall and Co. had forwarded him in a private communication a rough estimate of the cost.— The letter stated " With reference to the electric light- ing at Runcorn, after hearing our Mr. Hall's report, we are of opinion that the town is admirably adapted for electric lighting. We estimate that it would take about 2i5 arc lamps of 2,000 candle power each, and 150, ot 16 candle power, incandescent lamps to light the town. effectively ; the approximate cost of installing the same would be something under £4,000. We do not, how- ever, bind ourselves to these figures. We should be pleased to fit temporarily 2 are lamps in any of your main thoroughfares where the use of an existing steam or gas engine could be acquired for say a fortnight so as to demonstrate to your Board and ratepayers the vast superiority of electric light over gas.; should you decide to place the contract with us we would not make any charge for the experiment ; on the other hand, the cost would not exceed £25. Supposing the complete installa- tion should cost £4,000, the annual expenditure, includ- ing repayment of principal and interest repairs, renewals, Sm., would be £548. At the present time the Commissioners were paying about £B2O a year for light- ing the town by gas. He (Mr. .Huttplireys) would assume that Mr. Hall had taken too small an estimate of the requirements of the town for lighting purposes, and he haa added to his estimate another £2,000 for installa- tion and also another £lOO for increased maiatenance, and that would bring up the estimated annual expendi- ture to £750, so that even with that additional £2OO per year added there was still a saying of something like £7O a year j besides haying the benefit of the improvement. This eitimatS ilia:Judea 3 duplicate plant, the advantage of which must be odvious, first in the ease of a break- down, and secondly the smaller plant might he worked during the day time while the larger plaut took increased work at night. He thought the'Coultrilto.eners might safely undertake tO supply any private individual in the town with the electric light at a tteee at all events not exceeding what thet were now rfaying for gas and at the pine time deriVe a very handsome profit, Which mitln'o go for the benefit of the ratepayerS. M. Meadowcroit agreed that a case had been rnade out for inquiry, but there seemed to he a weakness in connection with the matter which it was desirable should be overcome. And that was so far as Mr. Hall was concerned. He did not know whom that gentleman represented, but it was desirable, that they mould have independent testimony, and he suggested that, as there was no immediate hurry, tb engagement at Mr. Hall to prepare estimates, &c., should be postponed until the Commissioners had obtained information from some of the authorities who had prac- tical experience of the working and cost of time electric light.—Mr. Humphreys accepted the suggestion, which was supported by Mr. Taylor, who also pointed out that at Blackpool the electric light was only used in the sum- mer, and was then put out at midnight, so that it most not be practicable or too expensive for lighting the town. —Mr. Humphreys replied that:at the present time Black- pool were promotiue a bill to enable them to spend £26,000 in lighting the whole town with electricity.— Mr. Taylor said if it were found that they could save anything, and have a better light by means of electricity, he should do all he could to promote it.—Mr. ltisbrown intimated that the electric light was in operation at Prestcn.--The recommendation as amended, was then carried. THE AFFAIRS OF THE WIDNES ALKALI COMPANY.

The hearing of this motion took place in the Chancery Court of Lancashire sitting in Manches- ter on Tuesday before Mr. W. F. Robinson, Q.C., acting Vice-Chancellor. It was a motion arising oat; of the voluntary liquidation of the Widnes Alkali Company. Mr. Ledford and Mr. Astbury appeared for George Yates Bolton and Elizabeth Bolton, who were holders of shares in the company. Mr. Hotch and Mr. Hughes were for Mr. C. E. Bar- low. and Mr. Reberlev and Mr. Lawrence for Mr. G. U. Bolton and Mr. J. R. Wild, who, with Mr. Bar- low, were directors of the company. The motion had reference to a sum of .245,000.—Mr. Radford said the Widnes Alkali Company was incorporated , in 1865 under the Companies Act, 1862, with a capi- tal of £20,000 in 2,000 shares. Overtures were made in the early part of 1890 by the Chemical Union for the purchase of the business of the com- pany for £315,000. A meeting of the shareholders was held on the 15th of January: 1890, at which a rest); lotion was carried unanimously agreeing to Oen for iF.:345,000. It was also resolved that in the event of the sale being completed a sum of £45,000 should be given to the three directors for " loss of position." The sale was elTected, and the Chemical Union had since become the United Alkali Company. The motion was that the £45,000 paid to the directors should be returned to the mhareholderA of the original company. Mr. Radford contended that the resolution in no way bound his clients, because the money was not bona-fide voted by way of compensation for loss of position, being far be- yond what the directors would have been entitled to even if they had a legal claim against the company for wrongful dismissal. Mr. Barlow and Mr. Wild had since become directors of the United Alkali Company at the same salary as they received from the old company. viz., £1,500. The purchase mosey had been paid in shares of the new company, distributed so far as £300,000 was concerned pro rata among the share- holders of the old company. The £45,000 divided amore the directors was also in shares.—The case was adjourned. The case was resumed on Wednesday, when the court was occupied all day in hearing documentary evidence in the case, which is expected to last for several days.

PROPOSED RESTORATION OF FARNWORTH PARISH CHURCH.

The annual congregational tea party and concert in connection with Farnworth Parish Church was held on Friday evening last week, under the presi- dency of Mr. John Brock, J.P. The Vicar, the Rev. J. Wright Williams,delivered a speech, in which he advocated the restoration of the old fabric of the church, and the Chairman in the course of his speecit dwelt at length on the same topic. He said for the past four or five years they had often heard of church re- storation, and no doubt something had been dem in the direction of church repairs, but not church restoration. , What had been done only touched the fringe of the sub- ject, therefore something very much more thorough ought to be done, and he thought now that they had built and kept out of debt two churches, and also built a vicarage, which they hoped would soon be free that debt, the mother church might look for help. He would be disappointed if the daugh- ter churches, St. Ambrose and St. Paul's did not assist them most cordially in this matter. He • had faith that they would. If an appeal were made to the public about the church restoration there must be a complete plan to work upon. It was no use going to the p eople and asking them for their money unless t could be pointed out to them what was going to be done. Restoration did not mean pulling down everything old. and putting in everything new, bat the improving, and in some cases the removing,of excrescences and portions of the church that never ought to have been there. fie referred chiefly to the galleries. No doubt it was neces- sary to find increased accommodation in the parish church at one time, but now that they had the churches of St: Ambrose and St. Paul be thought the seating room in the galleries could at once be done away with. Architecturally they were an excrescence and an eyesore. The restoration could take place in sections as funds were forthcoming, and it would probably take two or three, perhaps five, years to do the work. It would mean the banishing of pews from the chancel—pews ought not to be there—and putting the choir in their proper place; also reseating the nave, and making pro- I Vision for a larger organ to be put downstairs.

JUDGE FFOULKES AND THE NEV

FORM OF OATH.

At the Warrington County Court, on Thursday morning,bis Honour Judge. Ffoulkes,said he wished to allude to an incident which occurred last court day, about which there seemed that some strange misconception had got into the papers. What took place was this. Mr. Jenkins asked to be allowed to take the oath according to the Scotch usage, with the right hand raised, and without kissing the book. He (the Judge) asked if he had some conscientious objection to the oath, and he said it was not that,but he wished to be excused because of the epidemic. Mr. Jenkins told him of some decision which apparently dealt with the subject. He replied that he could not recognise the deci7ion in the face of well-established usages, but that he was net obliged to kiss a particular °Testament, if he preferred another. He (the Judge) quite forgot at the time the Act known as " Bradlaugh's Act," which was pass.d. irl 1888, and had Mr. Jenkins mentioned it and asked to be sworn in that way he would have been very glad indeed to have allowed him to do so. Apparently (so he had been told, for he had not seen the papers), he had refused to allow Mr. Jenkins to take the oath with up- lifted hand, because he had no scruples against taking it in the ordinary way. He did not know how that got about, but it was an entire misconception of what passed. What was said about religious scruples took place previously. He thought Mr. Jenkins had conscien- tious objections to taking the oath, but in answer to him (the Judge) he said he only wished to be excused Owing to the epidemic. He should be glad if Mr. Jenkins was in court to hear what he had to say.—Mr. Longton, solicitor, corroborated his Honour's statement. His Honour, in the course of the morning,aliowed a witness, Mr. W. I. Lawton. to be sworn with uplifted hand.

THINGS HEARD AND SEEN

AT WARRINGTON.

1 hear that there is some likelihood that before long the excellent and industrious agent for the Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Animals will have to be translated to another sphere of labour. This will come in the usual course of• things in accordance with which all the agents of the society, are changed about from time, to time. Inspector Dowty, too, has of late had his exertions somewhat suspended through having been an in- valid for some time, but has been doing good work since his recovery, as instanced at Daresbury Ses- sions on Tuesday. The practical lessons which Mr. Dowty has given in humanity to those who reward with brutality the poor animals that do them such substantial service hirte been numerous in this dis- trict, and the seed he has sown will, I am sure, not have been thrown away. The fact that be has al- ready, I believe, considerably exceeded the usual period for removal of an agent shows that his ser- vices are appreciated at headquarters, and when the time comes for his departure we shall all feel sorry to lose so zealous and impartial an auxiliary in so good a cause. - - -

-- I have now and then heard, especially in con- nection with the aged folk's treat, speculations as to who was the oldest inhabitant of Warrington, but I have never heard the credit of the distinction of being such given to him whom I believe is the right person. Mr. Adam Campbell, of Brick-street,, who will be 92 years in April next, is, I think, the oldest man in the town, and, I fancy, its oldest in-. habitant. Mr. Campbell has lived in Warrington a great numbar of years, and has been seen going about until quite lately—since when, on account, of the severe weather, I suppose he has been keep-. ing indoors—in a. hale and hearty condition. They old man's eyesight and hearing are remarkably good, considering his age. His mental faculties also, are clear, and not long since he was absolutely carrying on the business of a coal agent, and can— vassing for orders for coal, and probably soon will be again, now that the weather has become more: favourable.

The old gentleman is said to be quite an author- on the scriptures. Some time ago the Rector of Warrington preached a sermon on the passage in .which the Psalmist names three score years and ten as about the maximum of human life,and remarked that it was a curious fact that David died himself just at that age. Mr. Campbell on hearing from a triend that Mr. Willis had made this statement, declared there was no authority for it in the Bible, and after a careful search said he was quite con- vinced there was no record as to the date of David's death.

The new oath—not affirmation as some are pleased. to call it—bids fair to become the accepted form of swearing in a ceurt of law. It was rather an augury in this direction to see three medical men, the privilege of being at the Warrington Police Court ,the other day, ill carry with it eovin,eo:anftieiri tahnisotmhaetrincelanimTinhge fact w . a strong argument to the public mind that the , objection to kissing the back of the Testament which has received the osculatory impresses of all and sundry is not so fanciful as some might imagine it to be. The new ceremony is much cleaner, and to my mind more impressive than the-old one.

Judge Ffoulkes has been making an elaborate statement in the County Court, as to what he said or meant to say, when he recently refused to allow Mr. Jenkins to take the Scotch form of oath, and he says he makes the explanation to correct some strange misconception which had got into the Papers. It would puzzle a Philadelphia lawyer to find out after reading the reports of what took place,and the judge's explanations where the strange. misconception comes in. i can hardly see how, the different papers each giving its own independ- ent report, and all agreeing as they do, there could be any misconception, and the judge's own observa- tions show that there was not. The fact is his Honour has found he was in the wrong and has bad to make the amena'e hal orable in the best way that he could. MERLIN,

BEFORE THE MAQISTRATES,

AN UNPROVED CHARGE OF POACHING. At the Warrington Borough Magistrates' Court, on Monday, Wm. Fogg., labourer, 15, Factory Yard, and Joseph Shaw, 28, Powys-st., were charged with being in possession of game in Knutsford-road, they being suspected of coming from land where they had been poaching. Mr. Browne appeared for the defendant. P.C.a. 23, and Newey gave evidence. About 7 p.m, on Sunday, January 22nd, they encountered the defend- ants in Knutsford-road, coming trom the direction of Thelwall or Grappenhall, with their pockets very buiky Newey searched Fogs and found a dead rabbit on him. Shaw walked away, and P.C. 2.3 overtook him and asked him what he had under his coat and be said "No- thing." Witness told him to unbutton his coat and waistcoat and found on him a live rabbit quite warm. The men had neither nets, sticks, nor guns, nothing but the rabbits themselves. They were very rough on be- ing taken into custody. The men's boots were covered with clay,- as if they had come from land.—Mr. Browne asked who had smashed Shaw's nose, which had been much damaged.—P.C. Newey: It might have been done with a stick.—Newey added that the men were associ- ates of poachers.—lnspector Spinks said when the pri- soners reached the station they had clay and rabbit fur on their clothes. They at first told him to find out where begot the rabbits, but afterwards said they had bought them of a man at Frodsham.—Mr. Browne said the case was a bad one under the information because there was no seizure of the rabbits the constables hav- ing not kept possession of the rabbits, but merely felt them in their pockets. They had been bought alive by the men at Frodsham, for 3s. 4d., and one of them was suffocated in Fogg's pocket.—The Magistrates' Clerk ruled that Mr. Brovvne's point was invalid. The cases, however, were dismissed.

Was HE CoNvicren ori SUSPICION ?—George Donohue, a carter in the employ of Mr. Greenough, mineral water manufacturer, was charged with stealing three boxes of soda water, value 65., the property of his employer. From the evidence given it seemed that shortly before seven o'clock on Wednesday morning the foreman, Henry Shaw, went down to the end of the yard and saw pri- soner going into the storeroom. He followed, but lost sight of prisoner. Returning he went into the yard to where a vehicle laden with boxes of soda water was stationed. He found that one box was missing and on turning round he saw prisoner getting over a wall from Scotland-road. Shaw asked prisoner to get in the shafts ef the cart and help to pull it up the yard; but prisoner said he preferred to push behind. Shaw went to the shafts and then came suddenly back to the rear of the cart, when he saw prisoner thrusting two more boxes of soda water through a gap in some railings. He asked prisoner where the other box was, but he refused to state. Mr. Greenough was told of the affair, and pri- soner was given into custody.—Prisoner protested to the Bench that he did not know anything about the box.— The case was remanded until Thursday; but as nothing had been heard of the missing box in the meantime pri- sonerzas committed to prison for a month. CRUELTY TO HORSES. James Wilde, Brick Kiln Cottages, Runcorn, was charged by Inspector Dowty, R.S.P.C.A., with cruelly treating a horse, on the 20th of January. Mr. Dowty said the defendant had charge of the horse,which was attached to two boats very heavily laden with coal being drawn through the canal. The water was covered with very thick ice, which was only just broken, and the horse was struggling with its load, and on account of the ice and of there being a strong head wind blowing, was hardly able to drag the load. en the near shoulder of the horse there was an *ld wound re-opened, the size of a two-shilling-piece.—The defendant had to pay costs.—Joseph Hignett, Duke-st., Runcorn, was charged with a similar offence, on the same date. Inspector Dowty said the horse was draw- ing boats on the canal.and was suffering from a diseased fetlock,and ringbone,and from very great inflammation, and was quite unfit for work. The horse had been des- troyed, and the defendant was dismissed, having to pay costs.—Richard Jeffs, of Grappenhall, was charged with causing a horse to be cruelly treated, by being worked while in an unfit state, on the 16th of January, and Thomas Overbury, of Grappenhall, was charged with working the animal. Thompson, of Warrington, was charged with trespassing in search of game,on the 14th of January,on the land,on the Moss Side plantation, of Sir G. Greenall. Mr. Jenkins prosecuted. The defendant had set seven snares, and had on him three pegs and a nail to make holes with.—He was fined 103. and costs.—Benjamin Ogden, ship canal man, was charged with trespassing on the 22nd of January, in search of game, en land at Acton Grange, occupied by Thomas Ball and owned by Sir G. Greenall.—Mr. Jenkins appeared for the prosecution.- 10s. and costs. CARRYING A GUN WITHOUT A LICHNCI4.—Tom Barber, was charged with carrying a gun without a licence on the 7th of Nov. at Grappenhall. Be threatened to blow P.C. Millington'.s brains out with his gun if he did not stand back, on his demanding his license. He was fined 20.5. and costs. DASUOING A WIN-BOR.—A POOR CASE. —Frank Mill- ington, butcher, of Stretton, was charged with unlaw- fully damaging the window of James Howard, Whitley, on the 14th of Decernher.—Mr. Browne defended. The complainant gave evidence as to the damage to the window and Thos. Wilkinson was then called. He said he and defendant were coming home to Stretton, and on the way Millington said he would smash a window, and witness remonstrated with him, but he would do so. Witness said "Then let me get away if you are going to do that trick" and he went away about 50 yards. Then he heard Howard's window smashed, and the defendant came and jumped on his shoulders" like to knock him down," and said "I've done it."—Witness in answer to Mr. Browne admitted that he had had three pints, and that he did not see the window smashed. He denied having said he never heard it smashed.—Mr. Browne said his client declared that he never smashed the window, and there was no evidence to prove that he did. Here was a drunken man who admitted that he had three pints of beer before walking on with the defendant, and he walked on with him till they got to the lane end, where Millington stood talking to some cousins of his. Wilkin- son was in front, and when Millington overtook him he found him sitting in the snow in the middle of the road, singing. He (Mr. Browne) could prove that Wilkinson had over and over again said he never saw Millington smash the window or knew he had done it. There was no case to answer,—The Bench dismissed the case.

WARRINGTON COUNTY SESSIONS. WEDNESDAY.—Before Messrs. W. H. Bleckley and U. R.

Armitage. DELTNICENNESS.—WiIIiam Knowles, of Croft, John Kelley, Burtonwood, William Bray, of Rixton, were each fined for drunkenness. Too LONG LINGERING, LOVERS.—Rebecca Bond, a domestic servant to Alderman Francomb, J.P., was summoned for having on the 18th January, attempted to get in a train whilst it was in motion at Great Sankey Station, on the Cheshire Lines Railway. Evidence was given in the case to show that the defendant visited her sweetheart at Sankey, and he accompanied her to the railway bridge. She went with the intention of catch- ing the 9.30 train but was still lingering with her lover when the " iron horse "entered the station. She bade him a hasty farewell and" bolted" down the station steps to the platform. The train was then in motion, but nothing daunted the girl leaped on the footboard and tried to enter one of the carriages. She lost her foothold and fell to the ground where she was picked up by a Miss Ellis, in a slightly injured condition. The C.L.C. had taken up a prosecution on account of this, and were represented by Mr. P. Davies. The defendant, who pleaded not guilty,was represented by Mr. Browne. —Mr. Davies brought evidence to prod° the foregoing facts ; but Mr. Browne contended that the train was not in motion when the girl tried to enter the carriage, and that the Company had only brought on the proSecution on account ot a claim made by his client for damages.— The Bench inflicted a fine of is. and Us. costs.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, FEBRUARY 4, 1893.

WIDNES PETTY SESSIONS.

FRlDAT.—Before Messrs. J. C. Gamble and J. Birchall. DRUNKENNESS—MichaeI Burk, Peter Turner, William Moran, Henry Jones, and Patrick McCabe were each fined for drunkenness. ST. HELENS BOYS AND WARRINGTON CAM/S.—Five boys from St. Helens named William Whyatt (17), William Johnson (15), John Crabb (13), Philip Murkie (14), and Joseph Broadwick (13), were charged with stealing a number of cakes from a boy named Hassal, of Bostock- street Warrington. The boy, who was employed by Mr. Allen, baker, Parker-street, was in Warrington-road, Bold, when the four defendants came to him. He was than carrying a basket of cakes. The defertdants threatened to kill him and throw him into a ditch if he did not give them some cakes. He gave them three but they stole more from his basket,and ran away: P.C.Foster was informed of the affair and overtook the youths at Sutton. Whyatt and Johnson were fined ss. each, and , the other prisoners were ordered to be birched. STEALING TIMBER.—Ann Wilson, 13 years of age was charged with stealing a quantity of wood value is. 6d. from the Widnes United Alkali Co. ; and Mary Walton, her mother, was charged with aiding and abetting her, From the evidence given by James Wilson, one of the company's watchmen, it appeared that both defendants came to some drainage work which was being constructed by the company, on Tuesday night. The mother was seen to point out to the girl several pieces of timber which were lying about, and the girl at her instigation picked them up and took them home to Widnes Old Marsh. The Bench discharged the girl and fined the mother 13s. or in default seven days' imprisonment.

RUGBY. WARRINGTON v. LANCASTER. This return match was played at Lancaster on Satur- day afternoon. The first game hal resulted in an easy victory for our local club, and the consistent good form displayed since pointed to them as being the probable . .

winners in the last match. But the " glorious uncer-

tainty " made its appearance, and Warrington in the

end were defeated by a dropped goal and a try to a try. A more wretched exhibition can scarcely be conceived.

Rough scrambling, forward play, accompanied by shady tactics, prevailed throughout the seventy minutes played, and the otiicial who had charge of the affairs, displayed his incompetency by allowing the Lancaster combination to proceel in their unlawful tactics wtih- out a word of reproach. _ _ _ . .

Winniqg the toss, Ashton played down the slope, facing a stiffish breeze. Turner failing to return Gibson's kick-off,the Lancaster men were soon in the ascendency, a free kick to Warringion bringing about a slight relief. Thompson and Webster exchanged kicks with no dis- tant advantage to either, and then Warrington wheeled a scrimmage, only to find that for their smartness they were penalized. Heesom frustrated a movement between Danby and Blea.sdale, but a forward rush, headed by Thompson and Kitchen, was neatly saved by Rarnes. Rough forward play claimed attention for a consider- able period, but a sharp passing bout by Evans, Bate, and Heesom tended to relieve the monotony. Berry, Holmes, and Thorniley were foremost in a dribbling movement, but a clever bit of play by Cowell and Bleas- dale sent Watrington back to their lines. Nevins and Jolley dribbled away, and Danby saved; whilst Bleas- dale had a shot ta goal, Barnes clearing. Nevins was again a conspicuous party,.and one determined effort reached the half-way. Evans intercepted a pass, but failed to travel far,the tackling being of a severe nature. Heesom spoiled Bleasdale when the Lancaster centre had a fine opening, but he preferred a drop,and a minor only was recorded. Uninteresting play followed the drop-out, Turner and Jolley in a rush leading up to Warrington invading. Again Lancaster returned to the attack, and this time some tall kicking took place between Webster and Bleasdale, the latter piloting across to Barnes, who failed to account for the ball, and Kitchen, and the litter ,scored. Bleasdale appar- ently placed a neat goal, but for some unaccountable. reason only the try was allowed. Warrington from the drop-out began to have a trifle the best of it, and after Jolley, Berry, and Teare had shown up well, the former in conjunction with Turner set up a permanent attack. Ashton got finely away from die line-out, and when Pressed passed across to Heesoin, but the fatal whistle ruled the transfer faulty, and a capital chance was des- troyed. Soon after half-time was called, with Lancaster leading by a try: Oa resuming the second half, the Warringtonians, aided by the elements, soon began to make matters warm for their antagonists. However, there was little regard for combination, and with the players excited above concert pitch, the several move- ments lacked the neat finish which are usually turned to account. Lancaster early on forced a minor, but from the drop-out Warrington pressed finely, and Boscow being to the fore with a fine kick, Nevins, Jolley, Ashton and Holmes forced Cowell to concede a minor. Turner ran finely soon after the drop-out, and prepared to pass when hen he reached the home custodian with bad results, for Lancaster relieved, and Thompson and Garth reached the half-way flag. Bate and Evans, des- pite the strict attention that was bestowed on them, got the oval away on one occasion, but the effort came to nought. Bate next dribbled with effect, and a pretty display of cross dribbling by Holmes, Bite, and Heesom saw the latter crossing over to Barnes, but Turnes failed with the difficult place. Lancaster, by forward play, reached the Warrington lines-once more, and after some exciting play in front of the Warrington posts, Garth dropped a smart goal. With this substantial lead the hornesters laid themselves out to prevent the locals from scoring, and shady tactics were adopted until the call of time, rendering the game hardly worthy of notice. Final result—Lancaster, a dropped goal and a try (six points) to Warrington a try (two points). Teams :—Warrington : J. Boscow, full back ; H. Webster, J. Heesom, and T. Barnes, three-quarter backs ; J. Berry, H. Teare, T. Donohue, J. lhorniley, W. Nevins, Fair. Turner, J. Holmes, H. Ashton (captain) and J. Jolley, forwards. Lancaster : Cowell, full back ; J. Lambert, W. Bleasdale (captain), and C.K. Thompson, three-quarter backs ; W. Garth. and C. Danby, half backs ; J. Lofthouse, E. B. Thompson. 3. B. Kitchen. J. Pinch, E. Caster, T. Gibson, C. Gibson, J. Herbert, and A. Garnett, forwards.

WARRINGTON " A " V. ;LANCASTER " A.' —A strong wind was blowing across the Wilcierspool enclosure when Lancaster commenced, and they at once worked their way in close proximity to the home goal. Monks, Eden, and Edwards caused the invaders to beat a hasty retreat, and a useful kick by Potts took the warfare further into the Lancaster lines, where Massey, from a pass by Lally, almost dropped a goal. Molyneux then exhibited useful work, some long exchange kicking beisig to Warrington's advantage. After one or two powerful raids and an eagerness on both sides, which accounted for several mistakes, Young got away finely down the field into the home quarter. Then Lancaster were penalized, but it was left to Massey to regain the lost advantage. A couple of fine bouts of passing by Hampson, Smith, Lawless, and Potts ensued, but the determined efforts of Redhead and Hartley foiled the attackers. Warrington here reissed a chance, and Gar- diner with a long kick transferred the scene of opera- tions to more desirable quarters. Redhead pulled up his antagonists and punted to Massey, who returned and followed up so swiftly that Hartley was almost too late in conceding a minor. Not for long did play stay in this vicinity, for some neat passing by the visiting backs almost resulted in Redhead scorixg, and the whistle blew for half time with a clear seore sheet. Warrington restarted energetically, Edwards gaining a lot of ground by charging a kick, and tackling Young at the quarter- flag. This was followed by Lawless and Potts putting in a neat piece of play, but Lancaster cleared again, Ashurst being tackled near centre. Strong runs by Gardiner and Redhead caused the play to settle near the home line, and for a time danger looked threatening. The Warrington forwards were, however, playing splendidly together, quick close packing and wheeling of the scrimmages being especially well executed, and a dashing dribble by Davies, Eden, Bailey, and Hampson transferred the warfare to midfield. Here Redhead was tackled, and after Massey had smanly scooped up the leather and ran down the touch, a scramble resulted in Lawless scoring. A grand bit of play by Lawless was supplemented by Randles, Bailey, Davies, and Molyneux putting in some Selling work, and Smith passing neatly to Potts, the latter rushed in. A fine dribble by Jones was foiled by Ashurst, a series of long kicking between the rival backs following. Again Lawless showed his smartness, and the splendid cohesion exhibited by the Warrington forwards caused Lancaster an anxious time of it. Another vigorous run by Potts ended in this player securing a fine try. Succesa did not end here, as

WIDNES v. Sr. Heums.—The above teams met on Saturday, on the Lowerhouse-lane ground, before about 5,000 spectators. THE SHIP CANAL.

A SATISFACTORY PREDICTION. The Manchester City Council on Wednesday approved a resolution giving the Ship Canal Committee power to take such steps as they might think proper in relation b 0 the bills before Parliament relative to the Ship Canal undertaking. Sir John Harwood said it was of the ut- most importance that the slight difference which existed at present between the Corporation and the Ship Canal Company, chiefly with regard to .representation, should be harmonised, if possible, before the bill got into the committee. They would want money to carry on. The, works were proceeding rapidly, and as satisfactorily as rapidly, and he was glad to say that when they got the report of the last six months' working it would inspire confidence in the minds of everybody who had to do with the Ship Canal. He believed it would be veil gratifying to the shareholders especially to know that the time was now thcught to be in view when there would be ships coming to Manchester, bringing the pro- duce of all lands. They were very anxious to see tll3t ' day, and anxious that the hopes and aspirations of the persons who put their money into this undertaking might be to some extent, at all events, realised. STORAGE ACCOMMODATION FOR SHIP CANAL TRAFFIC. I Mr. Marshall Stevens has issued a circular in which I he states that he is engaged in the preparations of a list of warehouses, mills, Scc., which are available ill the district for the storage of merchandise, and he asks that information may be sent to him with regard to properties which could be utilised for the storage of oversea traffic, stating the position, and showing the means and cost of access from the Ship Canal and docks, whether by water or by road. The question of the provision of necessary warehouse accommodation. Mr. Stevens adds, is now a very pressing one, and he desires that information should be sent to him as e trly as possible.

Having a vital interest in the trade of Hull, and having done something to increase it—(hea.r, hear),—he knew that almost every trade in Hull was In a position of peril at the present time. DEDICATION OF ST. PAUL'S PAROCHIAL ROOM, WIDNES.

The service of opening and dedicating the new parochial room in connection with St. Paul's Church, Widnes, was performed on Monday after- noon by the Bishop of Liverpuol (Dr. Ryle). The building has no pretensions to -,architectual beauty in its outward aspect, the style being known as the Free Renaissance. The materials used in its con- struction are common brick, and local red sand- stone dressing. In the interior the building is arranged with a central ball, planked on each side with five classrooms, divided by curtains, and these classrooms can thus be thrown together to form one large hall, capable of seating about 600 persons. There are also committee rooms provided, and at the west end there is a space set apart for infants. The place is well lighted and ventilated, and the floor is laid with " silent " wood blocks. Hot water pipes are used for heating the building. Mr. Paul Ogden, King-st., Manchester, was the architect, and Messrs. Sayce and Randle, contractors, the builders. A large company assembled on Monday afternoon. The Bishop presided, and on the platform were the Revs. G. H. Garrett (vicar), J. Wright Williams (Farnworth), Dr. Preston (Runcorn), Dr. Garrett (Manchester), Gibson Smith (Halewood), J. R. Glover (St. Ambrose), R. Micklejohn and W. Stanton Jones (Parish Church), Messrs. J. Brock, J.P., aud H. Wade Deacon, J.P. After a hymn had been sung, the Vicar offered a prayer, and the Rev. J. Wright Williams read a portion of scripture. The Bishop in declaring the building open,delivered a speech. He said such rooms had proved of immense benefit for parish work, and he sincerely trusted that one would be of lasting benefit to the people of Widnes. Since he was appointed Bishop of Liverpool, twelve years ago, he had always taken a great interest in Widnes, for many rea- sons—first because Widnes was the first part of the diocese he saw after his appointment, and also because it was one of the most hard-working towns he knew. fie had opened a good many mission rooms similar to the one in which they were assembled, and they all did a vast amount of good amongst the working class population. He would advise everyone of their clergy- men not to rest until they had got one of these rooms. He was one of those who advocated short services in their churches, independent of the Sunday services, hut there were many things which they could not do in a church and which made such rooms indispensable for church work.—Mr. H. Wade Deacon in moving a vote of thanks to his lordship, said they were much obliged to the Bishop for the great interest he had taken in Widnes, and he (Mr. Deacon) trusted that room would be a benefit to all the people of Widnes and not only to church people. They were all glad to welcome the Noncon- formist friends who had attended that ceremony.—Mr. Brock seconded the motion, which was carried unani- mously.—The Vicar announced that he had received a letter of apology for non-attendance from Mr. T. Sutton Timmis. who enclosed a cheque for £2O towards the collection, which amounted to £3B 10s.—In the evening a public meeting was held in the Parochial room under the presidency of the Vicar,and there was a good attend- ance.—Mr. F. Neil, treasurer, stated that the total amount raised was £2,442. of which £2,321 had been expended, but the room required furnish- ing, and for this purpose £650 was needed. Mr. Wade Deacon, the chairman of the Build- ing Committee was anxious that the debt should be cleared off within twelve months, and to aid them to do this he had promised £lOO and Mr. Stewart Deacon had offered £3O. (Applause.) They had thus had a good beginning and he hoped they would have a good ending. (Applause.) Speeches were delivered by the Rev. J. Wright Williams, the Rev. J. W. Willink (St. Helens), and Mr. R. Lewis. The latter gentleman re- ferring to the debt, said the Tin:misses. Gossages, and Deacons were always giving, but a great many large fortunes had been taken out of Widnes. He did not know whether it was not possible to draw up a list of men who had taken thousands of pounds out of Widnes. Was it not possible to catch them even yet and ask them to wipe off the debt on that room? He thought they might assist in the work and thus perpetuate their memories, which were now in danger of dying out.— During the evening part songs, Sm., were rendered by St. Mary's glee party and Mr. C. Bate, and at the con- clusion of the proceedings votes of thanks were passed to the speakers and others who had assisted, on the motion of Mr. S. Deacon seconded by Mr. D. Brookes.— The collection realized £4 17s. Bd. --co.— THE MAYBRICK AGITATION.

. The Central News states that, with reference to a let- ter from the Baroness do Rogues asking permission for Sir Spencer Wells to visit Mrs. Maybrick at Woking, the following reply has been sent from the Home Office :—"I am directed by the Secretary of State to inform you that your daughter is under careful medical observation,and that as it wonld be contrary to practice to comply with your request, he must decline to grant the permission you desire.—Your obedient servant, GODFREY LUSAING- TON." The Governor of the Woking Prison has also notified the Baroness de Rogues, adds the Central News, that as Mrs. Maybrick's life is no longer in danger, the , rules cannot be relaxed to permit visits.

At the Warrington Borough Court, on Monday Morning, before Mr. F. Monks (chairman), and Messrs. W. Pierpoint, J. W. Hayward, and S.Wright, James Daley, lodging-house keeper, 37, Scotland- road, was charged that he did not send notice to the Medical Officer of Health that one Peter Daley, his son, was suffering from an infectious disease. Mr. J. Lyon Whittle, Town Clerk, appeared for the Prosecution, and Mr. Forshaw for the defence. The Town Clerk said that Peter Daley was found by the Medical Officer in the defendant's house, suffering from the tat stage of smallpox, and in such a state as to be capable of spreading the disease. His (the Town Clerk's) contention was that this man had wilfully con- cealed the case in his house. There was reason to be- lieve that seven cases were traceable to this case. Mr. Norman was sent to make enquiry, and the defendant Pointed to an inner room as that in which his son had been kept. Nothing less than imprisonment would be an adequate penalty for this offence. It was his duty to give notice as soon as he became aware that persons Were suffering from infectious disease in his house. IRISH LINEN IRISH LINEN IRISH LINEN IRISH LINEN IRISH LINEN IRISH LINEN IRISH LINEN IRISH LINEN IRISH LINEN

THE LARGEST ELECTRICAL RAILWAY.

The Liverpool Overhead Electric Railway, which is to be opened by Lord Salisbury, is the largest electrical railway the world has yet seen, and America, popularly supposed to be the pioneer in huge enterprises of this character,must in this instance take "a back seat." The overhead structure is composed almost entirely of wrought iron, and is carried on columns, the total length being seven miles, and the total cost something over £OOO,OOO or £85,000 a mile. Nearly 25,000 tons of wrought iron have been used in the construction. At the outset the authorities had not made up their minds as to the motive power to be employed, but the direc- tors ultimately decided in favour of electricity, and placed the responsibility of this portion of the under- taking in the hands of Mr. Thomas Parker, the chief engineer and general manager of the Electric Construc- tion Corporation. Each train is to be made up of two or more carriages, each to seat fifty six passengers, and is provided with a motor at each end. The motors are so connected together as to be controlled at either end by the driver, who will carry a key, without which the motors cannot be operated. The quantity of energy can be regulated by the man in charge of the motor, and, although any practical speed can be attained, the trains as at present arranged cannot travel at a greater speed than thirty-five miles per hour, as at any speed greater than this the motor would deliver energy back, and so act as a brake upon the train. A novel feature of the railway is the use of automatic signals, electrically worked by the trains themselves. The trains will be lighted by electricity. There are thirteen stations, Uullf, upon iron airdore and colt-ming. the platform' fining about 115 feet in length by twelve feet wide, and three feet above the level of the rail. Access to the platforms is gained from the street level by four staircases at the more important stations, and on each platform a wait- ing-shed is provided, with pay-offices and turn-stiles. The cars are on the American bogie principle, and will meet the requirements of a very big traffic. rhey re all exactly alike, and contain compartments for two classes of passengers, with through communication from end to end of the train. It is claimed that the adoption of electricity as the motive power will mean economy, cleanliness, and efficiency. At the trial trip some weeks ago everything gave entire satisfaction, and complete success is prophesied for the first overhead railway in England.

MARRIED BY MISTAKE.

Mr. Justice Barnes gave judgment on Tuesday in a divorce case from Rochdale in which a curious point was raised. A man sought to be divorced from his wife, and it was urged as a bar to his plea that he himself had been guilty of misconduct by going through the form of marriage with another woman. It, appeared that the petitioner and his wife had signed an "agreement " to live separate, and the man considered in good faith that this document amounted in law to a divorce. When he found out his error he at once separated from the woman he had gone through the ceremony of marriage with him. Mr. Justice Barnes commented on the stupidity and ignorance of the man, but granted the decroe of divorce.

THE SCULLING CHAMPIONSHIP OF

ENGLAND.

The sculling race between Bubear and Homer on Monday, from Putney to Mortlake, for the champion- ship of England and £4OO, proved to be one of the most one-sided and hollow affairs of the kind ever seen on the Thames. The start, which had been fixed for half- past twelve, took place 13 minutes later, Bubear who won the toss, taking the Surrey side. Not even for a minute was there an prospect of a good race. Bubear at once took the lead, and gained so fast that at the Soap Works he must have been nearly eight lengths in front. The remainder of the race was naturally a mere procession. Bubear rowed on at his leisure, and easing down completely towards the finish was content to win by twc lengths.' '

MR. GLADSTONE'S DRIVE TO THE HOUSE OF COMMONS.

ENTHUSI A S TIC RECEPTION.

Mr. Glads—to-n—e's—niovements were awaited by crowds of people, numnering at least 10,000, nearly all well dressed, and quite orderly, excepting so far as the demonstration of the unemployed disturbed the general enthusiasm. When the Premier, accompanied by Mrs. Gladstone, drove out in an open carriage at half-past three from Downing-street, the cheering was tremend- ous. He endeavoured to escape public observation by changing the usual route, but the public got wind of this, and cheere 1 the whole way. The drive was through the Horse Guards' entrance and through the Park, and several thousands of people followed the vehicle, cheering all the way. The csachman made the whole circuit of the park, and came by way of Great George-street into Parliament Square. Here the Premier received a renewed ovation,and no unpleasant incidents occurred. The Premier looked pale, but cordially acknowledged the salutes of his admirers. The special police on duty, including the mounted men, numbered about 250. Observers of the crowds say that they were on the whole, most orderly, although by far the largest ever seen at the reassembling of Parliament.

A MANX CAT BEFORE A LEARNED

SOCIETY.

A Manx cat, the species of the feline tribe without a tail,must be a conspicuous rarity in France, for, curious to relate, a living specimen of the same was shown and made the subject of a special communication at a recent meeting of the Anthropoligical Society in Paris. M. Mortillet, who exhibited the interesting creature sans queue, observed that its race had been described by Dar- win, but so badly had this been done that there were serious doubts whether the distinguished naturalist had ever seen a specimen of the animal. It was pointed out also that on ancient designs and stamps in Japan there were to be seen forms of cats without tails or with caudal appendages similar to those of rabbits. Might not, therefore, the groups of tailless creatures known as Manx eats have originally been derived from Japanese ancestors? This was the query which the author sug- gested to the anthropologists of the Anthropological Society of Paris.—" Medical Press."

Feb. 28th. 1887. Society of Chemical industry (Manchester Section). I have chemically and microscopically examined Messrs. Crosby and Sons' " Cremoid " Calf Meal. I find it to contain albumininous substances which are necessary to build up the tissues of the animal system. The Meal also contains a considerable amount of oil, with carbo-hydrates, which tend to the production of fat. Nearly one-halt of the ash is made up of phosphate of lime (this. I need not say, is one of the chief constituents of bones.) From the examina- tion of this Meal, I eonsider it an admirable food for the fattening of calves, as the Meal contains all the elements for the building up of the flesh and bones of the animal body.
