Letters to the Obitor.

THE DISMISSAL OF THE UNION BAKER.

Sir,—The report in last Saturday's issue of the appar- ently niggardly treatment received by Mr. Cliffe from the Board of Guardians, calls for public comment and further explanation. For a workman who has become physically infirm after 25 years' faithful service, to be dismissed with short notice and without further pro- vision, mr_y be sadly too common in private business establishments, but when such a crime is committed by a public body like the Guardians, they must answer for it before the bar of public opinion. The question arises, have the Board power to superannuate Cliffs? If so, why don't they do something for him? The very fact that his successor Is to have three shillings more per week, indicates that Cliffs has bem paying a heavy pre- mium for a considerable length of time. A plainer case of purchasing one's own pension could hardly ever be. Those who have known Cliffe for the last few years will not be surprised at his inability to perform his duties ; trouble and affliction will tell their tale on the best of men, but it is a scandalous shame that such a worthy fellow should have been treated so much like a dog.— Yours truly, 4, Trinity Place, Feb. 2.

WILLIAM KNOWLES.

BISHOPS VERSUS BIBLE.

Sir,—The Archbishops of York and Canterbury ,Bishops Jayne, Stubbs, and others are condemning evening communion, whilst such champions of Bible teaching as Bishops Ryle, Bardsley, Stratton, and a few others practise it. So long as it is recorded as being instituted in the evening it ought to suffice to all loyal men, sup- posed to be biblical scholars (see Matt. 26c. 20v., Mark 14c. 17v., Luke 22c. 14v., the bour of the Passover ; Exod. 12c. 6v., evening ; Deut 16c. 6v. at the going down of the sun ; 1 Cor. 11c. 23v.,night.) In the time of Ambrose, Augustine, and Tertullian it took place in the evening. _ .

lam prepared to prove that it is the aim of the English Church Union party, with whom the first five named are in touch, to bring the Church of England back to the See of Rome, and evening communion is an inseparable barner,as the laity would not fast till 8 p.m., neither will the Papacy accept them with it ; nor will they give up their early Mass. To the law and to the testimony. The Rev. J. E. Riddle's Christian Antiquity ably exposes the unsourad dogma of such men as Dr. Jayne, and C. A. Lane, the Church Defence lecturer.— Yours &c., Frodshan3, Feb. 2, 1893.

R. FOLTLKES.

WARRINGTON ASSOCIATION FOOTBALL CLUB.

Sir,—Seeing a statement in your football notes of Saturday last, to the effect that Warrington Association had " chucked it," the consequence being that they were in a low state financially, as well as having a lack of energy, I beg to contradict this as inaccurate. In the first place the said club had two teams play- ing at the time the matter in question was in cir- culation viz. :—lst team versus Wilmington Park at Arpley, and the Reserve team versus Orford Crescent at Orferd. They have neither "chocked it" nor intend to "chuck it." In the second instance the funds are not low though they might be increased, as also might any other club's finance be open for influx, as in prosperity we are assured of success. It is, how- ever, true that one or two of our playing members have left us, but it would be a poor organization indeed that depended en one or two men for their maintenance. We have in the past received a great amount of god advice and well meant criticism from your valuable paper, and have even received "tips " tending to our improvement which have been acted upon on more than one occasion, and we trust that you will continue to advocate in your journal the Association game in Warrington, as by doing this you greatly assist us. Apologising for trespassing on your valuable space and again asking to make this as public as possible in correction of the previous week's paragraph,—l remain, yours faithfully, JAS. MATHER, Hon. Secretary.

===== 0002601_18930204_art0101.txt | included=true | included =====
RE WARRINGTON A.P.C.

Sir,—As a playing member of the Warrington A. F.C. , I beg to thank you for the encouragement we have receiv- ed from the football notes of your valuable paper,during the present season. I notice, however, in your issue of January 28th that you state that the above club has "chucked up," and you give as a reason for this abandonment "that the members have become scattered, and the funds have become low." As one who is well acquainted with the condition of the club, I trust that in your next issue you will correct this state- ment, as the members have not become scattered, neither have the funds become low. It is true that one or two players have left us, but, acting upon the advice which has been given in your notes more than once, the committee on Saturday last called upon several of the reserve team, and with satisfactory results. Matches are arranged with the best junior clubs it is possible to find in Liverpool and Mid-Cheshire. This cannot be said of all the Association clubs in Warring- ton, and it accounts for what would appear to be in- terior results. I can assure you, on behalf of the mem- bers, that we do not intend to give up, but to persevere, and deserve success, even if we do not obtain it. Thanking you for the assistance you have kindly given us in the past, and hoping your favourable criticism will be continued, I am yours faithfully, A MEMBER.

WARRINGTON DEBATING SOCIETY.

DEBATE ON MR. GLADSTONE'S FOREIGN

POLICY.

On Thursday evening Mr. H. H. Dutton read be- fore the Warrington Debating Society, at the Masonic Rooms, Sankey-st., a paper on" Mr. Glad- stone's foreign policy." Mr. Longl and presided. Mr. Dutton commenced by saying that a few days ago speaking in Liverpool, Baron Be Worms, a member for one of the divisions there, made this statement 4. It has been well and truly said that Mr. Gladstone is the friend of every country except his own." That state- ' merit contained a great truth and a great untruth. It was perfectly true that Mr. Gladstone was the friend of all nations, especially if they were poor and weak, but it was monstrous to assert that he was the enemy of I his own nation. One would have thought that his price- less services to the country and the State during his long and illustrious career would have rendered it impossible for anyone to doubt his patriotism and have saved him from such a base insinuation. When leaders talked like that there was no wonder that the rank and file of the Tory party had mostly assailed Mr. Gladstone with scurrilous and disgraceful epithets. He had not the slightest objection to Mr. Gladstone being personally ' criticised and condemned by those who sincerely opposed him, but no one had a right to impugn his honour or his patriotism,and those who did so were blinded by -passion or politically mad. Mr. Dutton then enumerated the principles of foreign policy which Mr. Gladstone had laid down as those which ought to guide a nation, and which were substantially the fostering of the strength of the empire by just legislation and economy at home, claltivate and maintain the concert with Europe, to avoid needless and entangling engagements, Iknew- Ledge the equal rights of allnea bionst, the dthat g h love of freedom. -poeicv should always be inspir d y ,Dutton then reviewed the. hflrdeigneenpiOnliaCcycoredfaMnere, GladgCone, and contended that it. with tba.' octant character, principles be had enunciat.emd.—ln the succeed- .irg disc."„ sion,which was of a very p 0. NOTES ON GAMES AD

ArTILETICS.

FOOTBALL.

- Our first team visited Lancaster, on Saturday. Foe the second time the team of the county town inflicted defeat upon Warrington. However, this last defeat is by no means considered a fair one. The game of the Lancas- ter men was from beginning to end rough and unscien- tific, and almost the whole of the scoring was none by " flukes." To see the unskilled efforts of a Sunday School team is by no means interesting to a lover of the game. You have all seen the rowdy play of unsophisti- cated 12 year olds. Well, having seen that,you saw the game on Saturday so far as Lancaster played it.

From commencement to end the game was pretty much of a squabble; the home men often resorted to measures of a" shady "character, and when Warring- ton appealed against them the referee certainly did not put an end to them. This, at the outset, created a con- siderable amount of ill feeling between the two teams ; but, as the Lancaster men continued to" fluke," appar- ently taking advantage of the •' mildness" of the referee, even the Lancaster spectators couldn't restrain them- selves from criticising the childish stratagems of their home team. This match;if it was fruitful of nothing else, gave weight to agitation for the institution of properly certified referees.

I think it was Jack Point who said" alack and alas." Well, I cannot say "a lass ;" but I can say "alack." I say alack that our home team should have gone all the way to Lancaster, played the best game, been the best team undeniably ; watched their opponents belittle themselves in the eyes of all who saw the game; and then I say, despite all this, to come to Warrington beaten, in the eyes of the football world, by a drop goal and a try to a try. It ought, virtually, to have been tha other way about.

So far as he was able, Boscow played a characteristic game ; whilst the old Warrington favourite, Tommy Barns occasionally gave a refreshing display of the skilful agility, by which on more than one occasion he has made himself the "hero of Wilderspool." I mean of course a football hero. There is another hero in Wilderspool, but his heroic deeds are connected with an industry inseparable from the little village just be- yond the boundary.

The match between the Lancaster" A" team and our " A " team at home formed a great contrast to the play of the first teams. Both teams played with commend- able evenness and unity. Warrington were especially cowicuous in this respect,and they showed an improve- ment on their yecent play which I was glad to note. They are regaining their old style—are becoming their old selves, or, as Shakespeare said about his melancholy Danish Prince, " Hamlet is himself again." This last phrase is only an embellishment to the sentence,because, you know, it doesn't altogether fit in where a football team is concerned. Anyhow, our "A" team won by 0-4-4 to nil. Did someone shout "Hooray ?" Very well, then.

The Warrington Junior League Championship goes on apace ; or. rnayne I ought to say it has gone apace towards the termination. St. Mary's beat the Rangers by two tries to nil. This places the Saints on a par with the Parish Church, who, as will be seen from the following results, are the top " scorers" : For Agst. Plvd. Won. Lost. Drn. G.T Q.T. Pts. Parish Church 4 ... 3 ... 1 ... 0 ... 3 5 ... 1 3 ... 6 St. Mary's Rangers Latchford Stockton He ith 3 ... 1 ... 2 ... 0 ... 1 3 ... 2 1 ... 2 Walkers' Brewery 2 ... 0 ... 2 ... 0 ... 0 0 ... 2 5 ... 0

The dazzling, blinding, unequalled brilliance of the St. Helens Rees'. play this season, was smudged on Saturday with the dark shadow, of defeat. They met Salford in the cup conmetition,ancl,to use an expressive. though vulgar,phrase, were "knocked into smithereens." This is so sad to me. Week after week I have read the eulogies of one of our sporting contemporaries, until at length I believed the Recs, to be invulnerable, until I doted upon them as one of the only two teams who could play a certain gaine. The other team has not been found yet, and as the Recs. were beaten by six points to nil, I must lose faith in that journal and give up my ideal. "They have been too previous."

ASSOCIATION.

In our correspondence column there is a letter from a " Member " of the Warringtm A.F.C. He complains that in my notes last week I was incorrect in stating that there was a prospect of the club "Chucking up." He says that my remark that "the members are scattered" is not correct, also that the funds are not low, as I stated. Then he adds "It is true that one or two players have left us, but acting on the advice which has been even in your notes more than once, the Commit- tee on Saturday last called upon several of the reserve team with satisfactory results."

Well, I am glad the Committee called upon the reserve force, for I knew there were some good players there. But why did the Committee call upon severad. Simply, I presume, to supply the places of the playing members who have left—an action on their part which justified me in saying they were "scattered." With regard to the assertion that the funds are not low, I can only say this, that I know positively that the funds are not high. I know this, also, that the general tone of my notes last week was to the effect that the club was in peril in con sequence of a split.

I encouraged the remaining membe rs of the club to stick together and work with united action, and I doubted net that they would meet with the success which they undoubtedly deserve. As an admirer of the game, and of that particular club, I reiterate that advice, and I can only say, that if the club's safety is not so much imperilled as I thought "so much the bet- ter." The club has my hearty wishes for its success.

By the way, I learn that amongst some new fixtures which the W.A.F.C. have arranged there is one for next month with the St. Elphins. This surprises me some,as the American boy said. The W.A.F.C., tried to arrange a fixture with St. Elphin's some months ago, but, lain informed, a local clergyman connected with the latter team deemed it inadvisable that the two teams should meet, as he was afraid there would be a fight and some broken heads. If that is so, how is this peace to be accounted for ? Is it metaphorically speaking, that the tender lamb has lain down with the lion ? and if so has it been found out which is the lion ? I think when the fixture comes on we shall find transformed, the Elf into a lion.

===== 0002601_18930204_art0104.txt | included=true | included =====
THE WARRINGTON TRADES AND LABOUR COUNCIL.

The monthly meeting of the above Council was held on Tuesday at the Iforsemarket-street Cocoa Rooms, there being a large attendance of delegates. A discussion took place upon a previous motion, as to the formation of a Board of Conciliation to be carried out on similar lines to the one in existence in Liverpool. After a good deal of debate, it was resolved ; "That the Council deem it advibable that . . .

a Board of Conciliation should be formed in the borough of Warrington, and that the secretary be instructed to communicate with worship the Mayor, with a view to bring about a conference of employers and representatives of the workmen in the town." Communications were read from the Coventry and Bristol Trades Council. The former contained a resolution to the effect that it was considered necessary that some scheme or system should be devised whereby all questions of national importance could be under discussion by all Trades Councils simultaneously, so that all petitions—if such were required—could be presented together, thereby giving greater weight to the representations made. The communication from Bristol drew the attention of Trades Councils throughout the country to the action of the Aattiorities on the occasion of a labour demonstration, which was held in Bristol on the 23rd of December. and condemning it as a direct violation of the constitutional right of all citizens, and likely to establish a precedent that must redound to the disadvantage of the organised workers of this country. The Council unanimously passed the following resolution :—" That this Council deplores the action of the magistrates of Bristol, in obtaining the presence of the military on the 23rd Dec- ember last,as by their doings the city has been discredited before the country, and an unjust imputation made against the Trades Unionists of the city, This Council, therefore, respectfully requests the Home Secretary to appoint a commissioner to inquire and report as to the justification of the magistrates in taking the action they did.

===== 0002601_18930204_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
MAGAZINES, &c.

The February midwinter number of the century is very strong both as to text and illustration. The frontispiece is a portrait of Tennyson engraved by T.Johnson, from a photograph by Mayall,and on the reverse of it is a couplet from Locksley Hall, writ- ten by Tennyson so lately as August, 1892., Accom- panying the portrait is a very readable article on "The Voice el Tennyson," by the Rev. Dr. Henry Van Dyke, with reminiscences of a visit to the poet during the past summer. In addition there are two separate pictures—the work of American artistes—one by William Thorne called " Purity," and the other by Mrs. Mary [hillock Foote, called "The Mourning Dove." Besides the hign-class serials,there are five short stories,each:one well writ- ten and interesting. Perhaps the most notable article of the number is a paper entitled " A Voice for Russia." by the Secretary of the Russian Lega- tion in Washington. Lovers of verse are also pro- vided for, as well as lovers of art ; in fact the num- ber is full of variety and good things. Cassell's " Magazine of Art" contains some powerful pictures, the first one a copy of" La Zingarella," by Luke Fildes, HA.; a charming pas- toral scene "The sound of the Sickle," from the painting by Tom Lloyd, a series of sketches illus- trating the article, "The Art Life of John Leech," by Henry Silver, and a number ot others, each one beautiful, not the least interesting being a series of drawings by Walter Crane. The current issue of " Cassell's Magazine " is as good as its predecessors. and still holds its own as being one of the most popular magazines of the day. It contains an article for ladies on " Ribbon Work," another on "What to wear in February," and a "Cookery experiment," all equally interesting to the fair sex, besides the interesting serials, and a number of miscellaneous papers both instructive and amusing. The "Quiver "is another equally popular maga- sine, fit to be read in any home by anybody, and "Little Folks" is still the favourite for the younger members of the family, eagerly expected and welcomed each month.

Along with these we have the " Dore Gallery," "Don Quixote," illustrated by Dore ; " British Ballads" which has reached No. 11; "Familiar ild Flowers "with its beautifully coloured plates ; the " Portrait Gallery " containing three photo- graphs of celebrities by W. and D. Downey ; "Science for all " ; " Figoier's Scientific Works " ; "Storehouse of information "; " Natural History"; "British Battles on Land and Sea"; and a sixpenny edition of "Penny Illustrated Stories,"all published by Cassell and Co. in their usual artistic and highly finished manner. _ _

Mrs. Weldon again pleases the ladies with her " Journal of Dress and Fashion ; " the pattern this month being an exceedingly pretty cape,very much in demand just now,and easy to make from so good a model. There is the usual coloured plate,and a variety of articles all interesting, such as on how to fold finger napkins,the skirt for 1893, hints on etiquette, care of the eyes, &c.. SEc " Weldon's Practical needlework " contains thirty-seven engravings, showing original designs and stitches used for the new Hungarian embroidery, suitable for chair- backs, wall pockets, bed spreads, tea cosies, night- dress and brush and comb sachets,and other articles both quickly and inexpensively worked. Macmillan's " History of the English People" has reached the period of the Reformation,and contains a number of well-finished pictures, illustrating the " England of Queen Elizabeth." A "Souvenir of King Lear" presented by Henry Irving at the Lyceum Theatre, and published at the London offices of "Black and White" for one shil- hng, is a charming book for the table, and the illustrations make the tragedy seem very real, the "get up" of Henry Irving and Ellen Terry being most characteristic.

===== 0002601_18930204_art0107.txt | included=true | included =====
DEPARTURE OF THE REV. F. K FREE- STON. FAREWELL SERMON.

On Sunday morning, at the Unitarian Chapel, Cairo-st., Warrington, the Rev. F. K. Freeston, pas- tor, who is leaving the town, preached his farewell sermon. There was a large congregation. The rev, gentleman took for his text Matthew vi. 10, "Thy kingdom come." In concluding his discourse he said : And, now, my friends, you must know my reason for taking this mighty theme this morning. I cannot forget, however much I would, that this is the last Sunday of our settlement here together, and that when once this day is over, I can never again address you from this pulpit as your minister. But I long to entirely sink myself in the sense of our common life,and to leave behind instead some speaking word which shall , preach for itself and linger on long after I am gone. Overwhelmed as I am with the thought of the vast and limitless Truth, I humbly feel how little, how feeble are the faltering efforts of men. When you speak of any small success which has happily blessed my work in your midst, I can only feel as did St. Paul. "Neither is he that planteth anything, nor he that watereth, but God who giveth the increase." Yet I want to leave with you ' one helpful word. And as I call back the study hours of the past four years, in which I have pondered what I should say to you Sunday by Sunday, and wondered whether it was helping you in any real way, and endea- voured so to express my thoughts as to find some entrance into your hearts, I realize again how all along one great idea, and one deep desire, have prompted all I have tried to say : that root idea was the Kingdom of God that great desire "Thy Kingdom come." And this, therefore, is the parting word that I would wish to leave with you, as really covering everything that either has or can be said ; lay hold of Christ's grand ideal of the on-coming Kingdom of God. Believe in it, rely on it,begin with it, end with it. Let this be your daily inspiration : lam helpiag on the Kingdom. I know no higher, or amp- ler thought. It includes all other things, it puts every- thing into its right relation ; it wraps it round with a mighty conception ; it is the Alpha and Omega of all religion. If it were permitted me to choose what you should hereafter say of my preaching to you, I should beg you to say." He preached about the kingdom of God." If 1 were asked to select a text to be re- membered in your minds as the keystone to Christ's religion, I should choose the prayer, " Thy Kingdom come." And, my dear friends, it will be to me a hallowed memory that you have joined with me so often in the utterance of that petition. At every service in this place during all the Sundays we have worshipped together, the prayer has been raised to heaven—Thy Kingdom come! And we have tried together—imperfectly, I know, but yet tried in our measure to help it come—to make this building a reli- gious home, where we recognised every man as our brctlier, Christ as our human exemplar, and God as our Heavenly Father. HOW far we may have succeeded or failed is known alone to God. Humbly we fay our work at His feet and leave to Him the result. But if by , our worship and fellowship here a single soul has been brought to God,and has entered within the kingdom,and found the higher life, then how great has been our pri- vilege, how deep should be our joy! Then these swiftly fleeting wars will not have disappeared into the un- known without leaving gently behind some parting benediction. To von then who have listened this morning so patiently to these farewell words, let me say as a last behest that which even more I say to myself—to individual faithfulness, yours, and mine, God has committed the coming of His Kingdom. The Kingdom comes in diffe- rent ways—in one way to one, in another to another, but be it yours to say " Let it come through me, let it begin here." Therefore lay no limit to your souls' free aspiration, fear no libel which the world may put upon you; respect these promptings as God's guilding voice, find your own share of truth, form your own idea of life, pray without ceasing " Thy kingdom come." But then remember that you are not a unit left to yourself to struggle alone. Round you are the beating hearts of fellow-seekers, fellow-workers, making in their different ways for the same final goal. For them no less than for yourself let your work and your thought carry out your prayer, " Thy Kingdom come ;" through them, through me, through anyone, only let it come, and then nearer and nearer shall that kingdom come from age to age, meeting the face of the young as a rising dawn and brightening the song of the old, "Lord, new lettest thou Thy servant depart in peace."

On Monday evening the annual meeting of the con- gregation was held in the schoolroom, when the reports and accounts of the different organisations connected with the church were considered and adopted. After- wards a social meeting was held in the same room. Mr. F. Monks presided, and the room was filled with mem- bers anxious to take a personal farewell of their pastor. Addresses expressive of the deepest regret at his depar- ture and heartiest" God-speeds" and good wishes for his success in his new sphere of labour were delivered by the Chairman, Mr. T. Birchall, Mr. J. R. Young, and Mr. J. H. Knowles. The Rev. F. K. Freeston, in reply, reviewed his connection with the church and the con- gregation, and with a full heart thanked them for all they had been to him and to his wife. He pointed out certain directions in which he urged them to continuous and increased exertion, and in taking farewell expressed the pleasure it would be to come down occasionally to preach to them, concluding with the fervent prayer, •' God he with you till v7e meet again." The addresses were interspersed with a glee by some members of the choir, a reading by Mr. Perris, and a violin solo, charm- ingly plaseci by Miss Birchall. Refreshments were afterwards dispensed, and the company indulged in social intercourse. At the close Mr. and Mrs. Freoston took a personal farewell of all present as they were leaving the room.

BY LIIKB BLUNT.

Opposite the Farmers Stores, the other evening I turned my head in the direction of the old Boiler Works. And something caught my eye. It was neither a gnat nor a brick; it was no missile from a thoughtless boy ; nor was it that dangerous im- plement handled by a reckless girl—an eight inch hat pin. To my scholastic mind it was a sight which had a tendency to " set my blood on edge and make my hares stand on hens." This is an intended joke by a classical author—one who wrote in a dead language. Suspended from the boiler works wall was a sign indicating that the Warring- ton"Amatuer" Athletic Club was held there. I had a fancy for going into this club, so I went through a two inch gap in the rails into the yard, and up the stairs to the door of the club room. I knocked ; did it twice ; and then a dark, tall, melancholy, wistful looking youth came. He looked at my hat and boots for a while, and then said he concluaed I had made a mistake. That place wasn't a mission house, and it weren't no go me snivelling round with any temperance tracts. I assured him I was not what he had taken me to he and besought him to descend the steps with me. I datermined to wreak my vengance on him.

When we reached the yard again,' pointed up to the side of the building and said r " Why, oh, why, have you so cruelly slung that bull out there suspended by nails and chains."

He twisted sharply round and pee Ted up ;he gazed for one short moment, then his eyes rested on my face, and he murmured something to himself and then said aloud "My poor fellow, thot is not a bull ; it is a

"" Oh," I replied, " would you read it for me please." He again murmured to himself and then said aloud " It reads: Warrington Amateur Athletic Club." „

" Would you !dray tell me how amateur is spelled there "I asked, He said " A-m-a-t-u-e-r Amateur ?I see where the " bu:1" comes In. The last sentence in that word is always spelled " taur " not " tuer."

He forgave me freely and promised to get the sign altered and I went with him upstairs into the new "War- rington Amateur Club " room. It is the large room in the old boiler works which was formerly used as a works by the Burton Cycle Co. The club, which was opened a few weeks ago,consists of 120 members, including almost every well-known athlete in Warrington. It promises I t., be, in less than six months, one of the most popular and largest clubs for physical recreation in the town. Boxing, fencing, single sticks, weight throwing, an those kind of exercises comprise the " course " which members are to go through, and what more popular cluster of recreation can one find for young Englishmen What more beneficial, if conducted with proper desorum and system. The room is a large and spacious one. At present it is very plain and rather bare, but that is because there has not yet been time to equip it properly. At one end of the room there is a small dressing compartment, and near by was burning a large bright fire. Extending. along the sides of the room were seated some sixty per- sons, most of them well known athletes. In the centre of the room was roped off a 22 feet square, which I sup- pose, in sporting parlance would be described as a "ring." I sat down on a form and presently two ath- letes, in costume, with the " gloves " on entered the "ring." They met in the centre and shook hands, and then the M.C. said " time." They set to, and sparred away for about three minutes, when the M.C., watch in, hand again cried time. The first round was over;. the two combatants sat on stools in opposite corners and were fanned with towels. They set to again and had three more rounds, and then adjourned to the dressing- room. Two or three other pairs came and indulged in a similar combat. I was told that four rounds were the limit to each "do." There was nothing exciting in either of these combats; they were quite friendly affairs and from the occasionally excited look and un- scientific movements of one of each couple I thought that the pairs were rather unequally matched. I did not err, either, for I was presently told that during prac- tice it was the custom for a smart " fist" to take on a novice. 'This was done for the purpose of bringing the novice out.

"Yes," said my informant, "and I'll warrant it will! be an effective way of practicing. We are going to conduct all the exeteises, and if possible, equip ,the room on the most approved style. One day this week a deputation from our club, consisting of Mr.T. Grounds, Mr. " Fair " Turner, and Mr. Anderton, visited the Manchester Athletic Club, and the Orme Club, Manches- ter. They were shown round by Mr.Bellhouse, solicitor, who gave them all the information they wanted and some good practical hints ton. Mr. Bellhouse, you know, is one of the champion amateur boxers of the north. At his club they hold fortnightly displays of tournaments. We intend to do the game, and we have got some boxers and fencers amongst us who are quite capable of giving an exciting entertainment. We shall probably get some of the crack amateurs from Manches- ter, too. The displays will, to a certain extent,be public. We shall have the room fitted out snugly,and—but wait,. we are going to have a smart dust now. II ,---and E—have got them on together."

This was certainly a set too worth watching. The onlookers knew it, too, and throughout this combat,. like the-others, they never gave way to any loud oritic- isms, but watched in a way that spoke much for the decorum of the club. It was good to watch the two , combatants, for they are both recognised to be very I smart with the" gloves," and they are both men of sinew and agility. For four rounds they west a each other " ding-dong," and at the conclusion they were red in the face with each other's batterings,. and were panting and fatigued with their own lorisk exertions. There was a marked difference in the way these two men conducted themselves in the combat. One never guarded his face, that was F-, but after Sayer's style avoided most of the blows directed at his face, by alert dodgings of his head. And he didn't much mind the blows anywhere else. Th, other man, was the opposite. Be took all his punishment full in face ;he didn't seem to mind it either, and held his head stiff and erect all the time.

Dear sir,—ln reply to your letter-of yester- day's date, the following is the analysis of the gas drawn from the chimney at your Longford' Works, on May 17th, 1892 :—Nitrogen, 8610 per cent. ; oxygen, 13.03 ; carbonic acid, 3.37 ; carbonic oxide, nil; sul- phurous acid, trace ; hydrogen and hydro-carbon, nil. On evaporating the distilled water through which the gas had been aspirated no other odour was observable than that of sulphurous anhydride. THE "SILVER KO G."

The" Silver King" is a play which always con- tains a charm alike to Warrington audiences and those of other towns. The whole secret of the attraction in this drama is, in the first place, the noble and tender pathos of the title character, " Wilfred Denver, the Silver Xing," and in the second place the audience's interest is sustained by the ever changing situations and developments in the plot. The piece itself is a di cult one to do justice to, and therefore in the great success which Mr. Clifton Alderson's Company have won during the week, we are called upon to both congratulate the individual interpretations of the company, , and she skilful efforts of the stage manage- ment. The latter especially deserve credit, for the scenes in the" Silver King "• are many and varied, and unless dealt with in a prompt and alert manner are likely to cause serious delays. But about the actin. First a tribute is due to Mr. Alderson for his impersona- tion of the title-role it can simply be described as an al- most perfect interpretation of the dignified, sad, pathetic and oft-times passionate character around which the whole web of the play is woven. Miss Maud Digby as the wife of Wilfred Denby was also a great success. As the " Spider " Mr. Charles Chute was excellent. The remainder of the characters were well cared for.

COUNTY OF CHESTER. CONTAGIOUS DISEASES (ANIMALS) ACTS, 1878 & 1886. THE RABIES ORDER OF 1892. NOTICE IS HEREBY GIVEN, that in consequence of an outbreak of Rabies having occurred in the County, the following Regulations made by the Execu- tive Committee of the Local Authority of this County, at a meeting held at the Crewe Arms Hotel, Crewe, on Friday, the 9th day of December, 1892, are now in operation within the District under the jurisdiction of the Committee. SPECIAL REGULATIONS AS TO DOGS. I.—No Dog shall be allowed to be in or on any public place unless such dog is muzzled with a muzzle so constructed as to render it impossible for such Dog while wearing the same to bite any person or animal, but not so as to prevent such Dog from breathing freely or lapping water. Provided that the foregoing regulation shall not apply to packs of hounds, or greyhounds while being exercised or used for sporting purposes, or other sporting dogs or any dogs while being used for the capture or d estruction of Vermin, and in every case in charge of competent persons. 2. DOGS found in or on any public place, not being muzzled as provided in Regulation 1, or not exempt as mentioned in such Regulation, shall be seized by the police and detained and in addition to any penalties such Owner may be liable to far a breach of Regulation No. 1, all expenses connected with such detention may be recovered from the Owner of such Dog in a Court of Summary Jurisdiction ; and further that if such Dog is not claimed within three days from the seizure thereof, then it shall be slaughtered or otherwise disposed of as may be directed by the Chief Inspector of the County. 3.—Shows or Exhibitions of Dogs shall not be held within the District of the Local Authority except by License of the Local Authority granted in writing. REGULATIONS AS TO MOVEMENT OF ANIMALS FOR RABIES. 4.—No Animal shall be moved out of any Stable, Building, Kennel, Field, or other Place, if affected with or suspected of Rabies. s.—No Animal which has been in the same Stable, Building, Kennel, Field, or other Place, in which any other Animal affected with or suspected of Rabies, is or has been, shall be moved into or out of any such Stable, Building, Kennel, Field, or other Place, except on a License granted by an Inspector of the Local Authority, on a Certificate of a Veterinary Inspector appointed for this County. 6.—A1l Fodder, Litter, or other thing that has been in contact with or used for or about an Animal affected with or suspected of Rabies, shall not,be removed except by permission of an Inspector of the Local Authority. 7.—The Local Authority shall determine when Rabies has ceased to exist in an Stable, Building, Kennel, Field, or other Place. REGULATION AS TO CLEANSING AND DISINFEC- TION FOR RABIES. B.—All Utensils Feeding Troughs, Pens, Hurdles, or other things used for or about any animal affected with or suspected of Rabies, and all Carts, Vans, or Vehicles used for carrying Animals affected with, or suspected of Rabies on land otherwise than on a Railway, shall be cleansed, and disinfected at the expense of the owner, to the satisfaction of an Inspector of the Local Authority, in the manner following:— a. By the sweeping out and effectually removing therefrom all dung, sawdust, litter, or other matter. b. By the thorough cleansing thereof with a solu- tion of Carbolic Acid and boiling water in the following proportions, via :—Half-a-pint of Carbolic Acid well mixed with each gallon of water. REGULATION AS TO SLAUGHTER OF ANIMALS AFFECTED WITH RABIES. 9.—The Owner of any Dog or other Animal affected with Rabies, shall, on the receipt of notice from an In- spector of the Local Authority, requiring him so to do, forthwith cause such Dog or other Animal to be slaugh- tered ; but if the owner of any Animal other than a Dog affected with Rabies gives notice in writing to such In- spector that he objects thereto, the same need not be slaughtered except with the further Special Authority of the Board of Agriculture. REGULATION AS TO THE SLAUGHTER OF ANIMALS SUSPECTED OF RABIES. 10.—The Owner of any Animal suspected of Rabies, shall, on the receipt of notice from an Inspector of the Local Authority requiring him to do so, forthwith cause such Animal to be slaughtered; but if the Owner of such Animal gives notice in writing to such Inspector that he objects thereto, the same need not be slaughtered except with the further Special Authority ol the Board of Agriculture. REGULATION AS TO DISPOSAL OF CARCASES. 11.—The Carcase of any Animal that has died of, or been slaughtered for Rabies, or been suspected of Rabies, shall be disposed of under the supervision of an Inspec- tor of the Local Authority, at the expense of the Owner, by burying the same as soon as possible in its skin which must be first slashed so as to be useless, in some proper place,and be covered with a sufficient quantity of quick- lime, or other disinfectant, and with not less than six feet of earth. INTERPRETATION. Animals except where it is otherwise expressly pro- vided, includes, with the animals specified in the Act of 1878 (that is cattle, sheep, and goats. and all other rum- inating animals, and swine), horses, asses, mules, and dogs. Suspected animal means an animal suspected of rabies, and includes any animal which has been bitten by any diseased or suspected animal or which has been in the same stable, building, kennel, field, or other place, or otherwise in contact, with any diseased or suspected animal, or which has been otherwise exposed to the infection of rabies. Public place includes any street,highway,thoroughfa re, public bridge, royal park,public pari,garden,or pleasure ground,common unenclosed land, or other place to which the public have for the time being access: Carcase means the carcase of an animal, and part of such a carcase, and the meat, flesh, bones, hide, skin, hoofs, horns, offal, or other part of an animal, separ- ately or otherwise, or any portion thereof. PENALTIES. Any offence against the above Regulations is deemed an offence against the Act of 1878, which renders the offender liable to a penalty of not exceeding Twenty Pounds. DISTRICT. The District of the Local Authority of the County of Chester does not include the City of Chester, or any of the Boroughs either wholly or partly within this County. REGINALD POTTS, Clerk to the Cheshire County Council. Chester, 28th January, 1893.

THE EXAMINE SATURDAY, FEBRUARY 4,189 S"

THE QUESTION OF WELSH DISESTABLISHMENT. MEETING OF WELSH LIBERAL MEMBERS. At a largely-attended meeting of the Liberal members for Welsh constituencies held on Wednesday afternoon, at Westminster, a strong feeling of disappointment was expressed that the .question of Welsh disestablishment did not occupy a more forward position in the Govern- ment Programme of the session. Mr. Rathbone and other members, in reply, said they were led to under- stand that the position occupied by that question in the Queen's Speech must not be taken as indicating its order of treatment. A strongly-worded resolution on the sub- ject was, nevertheless, proposed, with the view of send- ing it to the head of the Government, but eventually its further consideration was adjourned, in order that Mr. Stuart Rendel, who presided, might have an op- portunity of conferring with Mr. Gladstone on the subject.

THE WNEMPLOYED AND THE OPENING OF PARLIAMENT. MR. LABOUCHERE'S ADVICE. Mr. Oldland, organiser of the proposed march to the House of Commons, on Tuesday received a communica- tion from the Chief of Police stating that the procession could not be permitted,and recommending that it should be abandoned. Upon receipt of this letter it was de- cided to abandon the proposed march. Notwithstanding this resolution. however, a number of men marched in procession along Eastcheap. A large body of the City police wereon duty,and followed the processionists down to the Embankment. Here one of the unemployed Committee named Waite attempted to address his fol- lowers. He was, however, prevented by the police from doing so, and the processionists were dispersed, ene man being arrested. Waite, Cooper, and Murphy, of the Unemployed Committee, then made their way to the re- sidence of Mr. Labouchere, at Queen Anne's-gate. They were met by Mr. Labouchere, and Murphy and Waite stated that they were out of work, and asked for his assistance and support in regard to the amendment which was expected to be moved last night in the House , of Commonsregarding the unemployed. Mr. Labouchere replied that they had better consult their leader. He could not promise them anything. The deputation after- wards attempted to hold a meeting in Parliament-square, but the police at once interfered and dispersed those assembled. Large numbers of police also assembled in Trafalgar-square, on Tuesday afternoon, in view of pos- sible meetings being held. An attempt was made to hold ; a meeting, but it was not permitted.

THE, HANSARD 'ONION PR,OSECTION. At the resumed hearing of the trial of Sir Henry Isaacs, Horatio Bottomley, and Charlia Denman, for alleged conspiracy to defraud the shareholders of the Hansard Union, to-day, the Official Receiver said he did not think the shareholders would get a farthing. In cross-examination he denied that it was.through animus that he succeeded in bringing about the. prosecution. He never said success would be a feather in his cap.
